**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates the detection-resolution limits of atom gravimeters utilizing large momentum transfer. It models the trade-off between enhanced phase sensitivity and signal degradation due to finite detector resolution, comparing standard versus 'mirrorless' interferometer designs. The key finding is that superior performance requires the detector resolution to be sufficiently high relative to the applied momentum transfer.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides quantitative limits on precision measurements in quantum sensing platforms (atom interferometry), directly addressing how practical limitations like detector noise constrain theoretical enhancements derived from advanced quantum techniques.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study analyzes the fundamental trade-off between enhancing gravitational phase sensitivity using large momentum transfer (LMT) in atom interferometers and maintaining high measurement fidelity against finite detector resolution.

**Main result.** Mirrorless operation only surpasses conventional interferometers if the detector resolution exceeds a specific threshold related to the momentum transfer; otherwise, the best achievable sensitivity is limited by the detector's Gaussian blur.

**Method.** The authors derive closed-form expressions for blurred output distributions and calculate the Classical Fisher Information (CFI) by analyzing how detection blurring convolves with the ideal quantum phase accumulation.

**Model / system.** The system models light-pulse atom interferometers, comparing standard Kasevich–Chu sequences against 'mirrorless' geometries using atoms in internal states. The analysis uses a solvable model incorporating Gaussian source and detection blur.

**Key observables.** Classical Fisher Information (CFI), Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), fringe visibility, and the minimum detectable acceleration ($\Delta g$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large momentum transfer factor ($n$), detector resolution ($\sigma_p$), interferometer time ($T$), and mirror asymmetry parameter ($s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes instantaneous, lossless $n$-photon pulses and Gaussian source/detection blurring; the analysis relies on approximations for the fringe-phase averaging.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate momentum distributions for different LMT factors (e.g., n=1 vs n=8) in the context of atom interferometry setups.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the QFI, then deriving the blurred output distributions via convolution lemmas, and finally optimizing the CFI to determine the resolution-limited sensitivity floor for various interferometer geometries.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Large momentum transfer (LMT) enhances the gravitational phase of a light-pulse atom interferometer by a factor $n$, while mirrorless operation with momentum-resolved detection can quadruple the phase-carrying quantum Fisher information. These gains compete in practice because the momentum-space fringe period scales as $1/n$, making the fringe signal increasingly vulnerable to finite detector resolution. We analyze this trade-off in a solvable model with instantaneous lossless $n$-photon pulses, a Gaussian source, and Gaussian detection blur, allowing continuous interpolation between Kasevich--Chu and mirrorless geometries. Closed-form expressions for the blurred output distributions and classical Fisher information are obtained, with a fringe-phase averaging approximation whose error is exponentially suppressed and agrees with numerical simulations at the $10^{-8}$ level. We find that mirrorless operation surpasses a conventional interferometer with the same momentum transfer only when $σ_p < 0.91\,m/(n k_0 T)$. Fringe-based readout exhibits an optimal momentum transfer $n^* \simeq 0.93\,m/(σ_p k_0 T)$ and a resolution-limited sensitivity floor $Δg \simeq 4.4\,σ_p/(mT\sqrt{N})$, independent of photon momentum. When this criterion is not satisfied, partial mirror asymmetry can recover part of the enhancement, whereas population-based readout remains insensitive to detector blur and ultimately favors the conventional sequence at sufficiently large $n$. Estimates for $^{87}$Rb sensors show that the mirrorless advantage is primarily restricted to short-baseline instruments.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously proves that using the iSWAP gate maximizes the spectral gap of the second-moment operator in random quantum circuits defined on any connected graph. It achieves this by employing sophisticated mathematical tools like Perron–Frobenius theory to compare the dynamics generated by arbitrary local gates against those generated by iSWAP.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous theoretical bounds on how efficiently quantum information can spread or mix across a network structure, which is fundamental for understanding quantum computation and quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to determine which two-local unitary gate ensemble maximizes the spectral gap of the Hermitian second-moment operator for random quantum circuits on a given graph.

**Main result.** The iSWAP gate was proven to maximize this spectral gap among all considered two-local circuit ensembles for connected graphs with at least three vertices.

**Method.** The proof involves advanced mathematical techniques, including analyzing the invariance of polyhedral cones under the iSWAP semigroup and utilizing Perron–Frobenius theory on associated block matrices.

**Model / system.** The model consists of random quantum circuits applied to a connected graph G=(V, E), where the dynamics are governed by local two-qubit unitaries acting on edges. The analysis focuses on the second-moment operator derived from these gates.

**Key observables.** Spectral gap ($\Delta(T)$) of the second-moment operator; largest non-trivial eigenvalue ($\lambda^\star$) and minimum eigenvalue ($\mu_3$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The graph structure (connectedness, $|V| \ge 3$); parameters $(\alpha, eta)$ characterizing local gate strengths.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the resulting average moment operator is Hermitian; comparisons are often reduced to analyzing specific boundary conditions or parameter regimes.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the text refers to equations and theorems detailing spectral gap bounds and matrix structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by first establishing the general problem of maximizing the spectral gap. It then reduces this comparison to analyzing specific mathematical objects (cones, block matrices) whose properties are proven using Perron–Frobenius theory, culminating in the main theorem comparing iSWAP to arbitrary ensembles.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove that the $\mathrm{iSWAP}$ gate maximises the spectral gap of the Hermitian second-moment operator on every connected graph with at least three vertices, among all two-local unitary circuit ensembles. We further prove that the polyhedral cone defined by asymmetric four-point inequalities is invariant under the transpose of the $\mathrm{iSWAP}$ semigroup, yielding a componentwise comparison certificate for its positive Perron--Frobenius eigenvector. These results resolve a conjecture of Kong, Li, and Liu.

</details>

</details>

**Summary.** This review summarizes how photoelectron spectroscopies have provided detailed insights into the electronic structure of lanthanide crystals. It emphasizes that surface effects critically modify the bulk properties due to the interplay between localized 4f electrons and itinerant spd states. The work highlights systematic research avenues for engineering novel correlated materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong correlation physics inherent in 4f systems—where localized moments interact with itinerant carriers—is a canonical problem in strongly correlated electron theory, relevant for understanding heavy fermion behavior and quantum criticality.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Understanding the complex electronic and magnetic properties of lanthanide materials arising from the interplay between strongly localized 4f electrons and itinerant spd-valence states.

**Main result.** Advanced photoelectron spectroscopies reveal that surface effects significantly modify bulk 4f-driven properties, enabling the engineering of novel low-dimensional architectures.

**Method.** The study relies heavily on advanced photoemission spectroscopies (PES), including momentum-resolved and resonant variants, complemented by XAS and XMCD techniques.

**Model / system.** The physical system is lanthanide-based crystals and intermetallic compounds. The physics centers on the competition between localized 4f moments (governed by Hund's rules) and itinerant spd conduction bands.

**Key observables.** k-resolved f-spd hybridization, temperature evolution of f-derived Fermi surfaces, layer-dependent magnetic anisotropy, and surface core-level energy shifts.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (T), crystal structure/coordination number (surface vs. bulk), and the ratio of 4f localization to spd itinerancy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that distinct physical contributions can be isolated from surface, interface, and bulk measurements using varying experimental probes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates radial probability distributions distinguishing localized 4f states from broader spd orbitals. Figure 2 shows a high-resolution PES spectrum resolving multiplet components in the bulk versus a shifted subspectrum from the topmost surface layer.

**Paper structure.** The review progresses from historical developments (1970s-1990s) to modern, systematic research lines focusing on specific phenomena like hybridization and magnetic anisotropy across different lanthanide compounds.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The interplay of strongly localized 4$f$ electrons with itinerant spd-valence states gives rise to a wide range of correlated phenomena and properties that place lanthanide materials at the focus of considerable research efforts. Beyond the bulk, their surfaces are of particular interest, where the reduced coordination, a modified crystal electric field, broken inversion symmetry in combination with strong spin-orbit coupling, and the emergence of surface states and resonances considerably reshape 4$f$-driven electronic and magnetic properties. This, in turn, enables novel functionalities of particular relevance for low-dimensional systems and their applications. This review summarizes how advances in photoelectron spectroscopies, together with improved crystal growth, have enabled detailed insights into bulk and surface phenomena of lanthanide-based crystals. After a brief overview of key developments from the 1970s to the 1990s, we discuss recent progress, focusing on systematic studies by the authors and collaborators that form a coherent line of research. These include the unveiling of $k$-resolved $f-spd$ hybridization, the formation and evolution with temperature of $f$-derived Fermi surface in Kondo lattices, layer-dependent 4$f$ magnetic anisotropy, the emergence of ferromagnetically ordered surfaces in systems with non-magnetic bulk ground state. This coherent line of research addresses core questions in the physics of 4$f$ systems and opens opportunities for engineering novel lanthanide-based architectures, including heterostructures and supramolecular complexes with novel physical properties and functionalities.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the sample complexity required to estimate key properties of unknown quantum states, such as their spectrum or entropy. By establishing lower bounds matching the difficulty of full tomography ($\Omega(d^2)$), it shows that characterizing these fundamental invariants is computationally hard. The methodology employs advanced techniques involving random matrix theory and moment analysis using Jucys–Murphy elements.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental complexity results that quantify how much information is needed to characterize a quantum state's properties versus learning the state itself, which has implications for quantum characterization protocols in any physical system.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on determining the sample complexity required to estimate or test fundamental, unitarily invariant properties of unknown quantum states.

**Main result.** The authors establish lower bounds showing that estimating the spectrum, von Neumann entropy, and rank of a $d$-dimensional state requires $\Omega(d^{2-\gamma})$ samples, suggesting these tasks are nearly as hard as full quantum state tomography ($\Theta(d^2)$).

**Method.** The proof technique involves constructing hard instances using sandwiched products of Haar-random projectors and analyzing the log-likelihood ratio via moment-matching techniques involving Jucys–Murphy elements.

**Model / system.** The analysis concerns unknown quantum states $ho \in \mathbb{C}^{d 	imes d}$, often constructed as mixtures derived from random matrix ensembles or products of Haar-random projectors.

**Key observables.** Spectrum (eigenvalues, measured by total variation distance), Von Neumann Entropy $S(ho)$, and the rank of the state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $d$ is the primary parameter; complexity bounds are shown to be $\Omega(d^{2-\gamma})$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on advanced mathematical tools, including concentration estimates (Lemma 7.3) and properties derived from matching moments related to Jucys–Murphy elements.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically proves lower bounds for three tasks—spectrum estimation, entropy estimation, and rank-testing—by constructing hard ensembles and analyzing the statistical distinguishability using moment analysis techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the sample complexity of estimating and testing fundamental unitarily invariant properties of unknown quantum states; namely, the tasks of spectrum estimation, von Neumann entropy estimation, and rank-testing. For $d$-dimensional states, and for every $γ>0$, we prove a sample complexity lower bound of $Ω(d^{2-γ})$ for spectrum estimation to constant sorted total-variation error, entropy estimation to constant additive error, and rank-testing to constant trace distance. Our hard instances are constructed from sandwiched products of Haar-random projectors, suitably normalized using a novel technique that lets us derive explicit expressions for high-order tensor moments of the resultant states. These moments can be expressed as symmetric functions of Jucys--Murphy elements of the symmetric group algebra. To show that two such mixtures are indistinguishable, we analyze the log-likelihood ratio and perform moment-matching, i.e., we set its low-order Jucys--Murphy components to zero. Indistinguishability is then obtained by bounding an $f$-divergence through the high-order components; the non-zero high-order terms and concentration of functions of Haar-random unitaries also imply separations in typical spectra, entropies, and ranks, proving all our lower bounds.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes optimal search strategies by extending random walk concepts from simple graphs to higher-order simplicial complexes. It models the dynamics as a mix between local movement and global jumps, using mean first passage times. The key finding is that optimizing search efficiency requires a specific, non-trivial balance ($\alpha \in (0, 1)$) of these two processes in complex structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on network science, the mathematical tools developed—analyzing dynamics across structured state spaces (simplicial complexes) using spectral methods and perturbation theory—are highly relevant to modeling complex many-body systems or emergent collective behavior in condensed matter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work extends topological measures, such as navigation time and optimal search strategies, from simple binary graphs to simplicial complexes to account for higher-order interactions.

**Main result.** For simplicial complexes, the minimum mean exploration time occurs at an intermediate modulation parameter $\alpha \in (0, 1)$, suggesting that optimal searching requires a balance between local random walks and global teleportation, unlike simple networks.

**Method.** The analysis uses mean first passage times calculated via matrix inversion ($Z_j = I - R_j$) and employs a perturbative approximation scheme in the modulation parameter $\alpha$ to study the asymptotic behavior of the search process.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled on simplicial complexes, which are geometric/algebraic structures capturing multi-body interactions. The dynamics are governed by a modified random walk operator $R = \alpha M + (1 - \alpha) D_s S$, where $M$ represents local hopping and $S$ represents global teleportation.

**Key observables.** Mean first passage times ($\langle T \rangle$), normalized occupation frequency, and the optimal modulation parameter $\alpha$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The modulation parameter $\alpha$, which controls the balance between local random walk dynamics ($M$) and global stochastic teleportation ($S$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a perturbative expansion in $\alpha$ (truncated initially) and assumes that simulation time is much larger than the Fiedler time for asymptotic results.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate examples of higher-order networks, the random walk process across dimensions, and plots showing how the mean exploration time ($\langle T \rangle$) varies as a function of the modulation parameter $\alpha$, contrasting network vs. simplicial complex behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the mathematical framework by generalizing random walks to higher-order structures (simplicial complexes). It then develops methods using connection matrices and mean first passage times, culminating in an analysis of how the optimal search strategy ($\min \langle T \rangle$) depends on the mixing parameter $\alpha$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The navigation time and optimal search strategies deriving from random dynamical processes on binary graphs have been extensively explored and analyzed, being of prominent interest in the network science field. In this work, we study an extension of these topological measures for simplicial complexes: a specific type of geometric and algebraic structures that encapsulates higher-order interactions. Here, the explorability analysis of simplicial complexes has been conducted in terms of the mean first passage times between nodes, i.e. the 0th-order simplices, with the inclusion of a long-range stochastic teleportation term modulated with respect to the local random walk hopping across the various dimensions. We also provide a perturbative approximation scheme recovering the modulation parameter between pure random walk and teleportation mode (for higher-order setting) acting as the expansion parameter.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theory for multilayer hetero-associative neural networks capable of storing exponentially many associations. By analyzing the network's energy landscape using advanced statistical methods, the authors derive an exponential capacity bound. The results are validated across diverse datasets, suggesting that the underlying geometric encoding principle is domain-universal.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical ML models, the analysis of complex energy landscapes, phase transitions (fixed points), and information storage capacity shares mathematical parallels with understanding order parameters in quantum spin glasses or disordered quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To model and analyze hetero-associative memory networks that exhibit exponential storage capacity, moving beyond auto-associative models.

**Main result.** The network achieves an exponential storage capacity ($P_c \sim e^{Nho_L}$) for mapping a cue to a distinct target, demonstrating domain-universal principles applicable from biology to language.

**Method.** Analysis uses cavity/signal-to-noise techniques and large-deviation evaluations of noise, extending methods from classical Hopfield networks.

**Model / system.** The system is an exponential neural network with L layers of N binary neurons, whose energy minimizes when every layer retrieves the same pattern index (hetero-associative state).

**Key observables.** Storage Capacity ($P_c$), Rate ($ho_L$), Generalization Error.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of layers (L), Number of neurons (N), Number of stored patterns (P).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The association must be a surjective function of the cue; generalization remains below memorization capacity, suggesting distinct capabilities.

**Figures summary.** The notes detail comparisons across various data types (Hidden Manifold, T-cell receptors, Language Intent) showing consistent performance metrics for capacity and basins.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the hetero-associative model to deriving theoretical bounds on capacity using advanced statistical physics techniques, followed by extensive validation against structured biological and linguistic datasets.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exponential Hopfield networks store a number of patterns that grows exponentially with the number of neurons, and in their classical formulation they are auto-associative: they complete a corrupted copy of a memory into the memory itself. Many of the tasks one wants such a network to perform are instead hetero-associative, mapping a cue to a different target. We introduce and analyse an exponential neural network of $L$ layers of $N$ binary neurons, each layer carrying its own dataset, whose energy is an exponential of the product of the per-layer Mattis overlaps, so that it is minimised precisely when every layer retrieves the pattern of the same index; the stored association must be a surjective function of the cue, and we show why nothing else can be stored at all. A cavity/signal-to-noise analysis, made exact at leading order by a large-deviation evaluation of the noise, shows that the aligned hetero-associative state is a fixed point of the zero-temperature dynamics up to a number of stored patterns $P_c\sim e^{Nρ_L}$, exponential in the layer size, with an explicit rate $ρ_L$ that grows like $L\log 2$; enlarging the basins of attraction lowers the rate but never destroys its exponential character. Comparing the theory with structured data we find that the exponential capacity and the predicted basins survive correlated, many-to-one patterns: the network is a near-perfect content-addressable memory. The same closed forms describe, without refitting, a synthetic manifold, real T-cell-receptor/epitope triples and natural-language intent data, so the mechanism is domain-universal. Generalisation to unseen cues, though significantly above chance, stays below memorisation, and it is the geometry of the encoding, rather than the data domain, that sets how far above chance it reaches. In this family, exponential storage and strong generalisation are distinct capabilities.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the energy cost of erasing stored information in DRAM cells by considering charge discreteness via the parameter $\kappa$. Using stochastic thermodynamics, the authors show that increasing $\kappa$ suppresses non-equilibrium heat loss during erasure. Consequently, the energy efficiency rises toward the theoretical Landauer limit, establishing $\kappa$ as the key control parameter for memory circuit efficiency.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete thermodynamic link between device physics (charge discreteness) and fundamental information limits (Landauer's principle), which is relevant for understanding energy dissipation in nanoscale quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how charge discreteness affects the energy efficiency of information erasure processes within Dynamic Random-Access Memory (DRAM) cells.

**Main result.** Energy efficiency increases monotonically with the charging energy to thermal energy ratio ($\kappa$), approaching the Landauer limit when $\kappa$ is large, indicating that $\kappa$ controls the fundamental efficiency ceiling.

**Method.** The authors employ a stochastic-thermodynamic model to calculate heat dissipation and energy efficiency across different regimes of the parameter $\kappa$.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a DRAM cell whose information storage relies on an integer number of electrons ($n$) on a capacitor. The physics is governed by the competition between charging energy ($E_c$) and thermal fluctuations, quantified by $\kappa = E_c / k_B T$.

**Key observables.** Energy efficiency ($\eta$), heat released during discharge ($Q_{	ext{discharge}}$), and heat released during charge ($Q_{	ext{charge}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionless parameter $\kappa$, the erasure error probability ($\epsilon$), and the Landauer limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a stochastic-thermodynamic framework, employing continuum approximations (Gaussian fits) in certain regimes to simplify calculations for $Q_{	ext{discharge}}$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how discharge heat decreases with increasing $\kappa$, while charge heat increases and saturates at the Landauer cost. Energy efficiency ($\eta$) is shown to increase monotonically towards unity as $\kappa$ grows.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by analyzing the energy costs of erasure in different regimes defined by $\kappa$. It shows that increasing $\kappa$ suppresses non-quasistatic discharge heat while driving the quasistatic charge heat toward the Landauer limit, thereby boosting overall efficiency.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A dynamic random-access memory (DRAM) cell stores information as an integer number of electrons on a capacitor, and whether this discreteness is thermodynamically relevant depends on the competition between the charging energy and thermal fluctuations. This competition is quantified by the ratio $κ$ of the single-electron charging energy to the thermal energy, and here we investigate how $κ$ affects the energy efficiency of information erasure in a DRAM cell. Using a stochastic-thermodynamic model of a DRAM cell, we show that the nonquasistatic heat released during the discharge step is suppressed as $κ$ increases, whereas the quasistatic heat of the charge step approaches the Landauer cost. As a result, the energy efficiency increases monotonically with $κ$ and approaches the Landauer limit where the effect of charge discreteness is maximal and the cell is effectively reduced to two charge states. The parameter $κ$ thus connects two thermodynamic regimes: a multilevel single-well memory, whose nonequilibrium initial state prevents quasistatic erasure, and an effective two-level memory that can attain the Landauer limit. These results identify $κ$ as the parameter that controls the fundamental efficiency ceiling of transistor--capacitor memory circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details the development of a sophisticated low-temperature, ultra-high-vacuum scanning probe microscope that uniquely combines atomic-scale imaging (STM) with macroscopic electrical transport measurements. This integrated platform allows researchers to correlate local structural changes or controlled material depositions directly with device performance metrics like resistivity and superconducting critical currents in 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state devices, the ability to precisely control surface chemistry *in situ* while monitoring quantum transport properties is highly relevant for studying emergent phenomena in engineered quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to establish a single, integrated platform that allows for direct correlation between local atomic-scale structural/spectroscopic measurements and global electronic transport behavior in two-dimensional (2D) devices.

**Main result.** The authors developed a versatile low-temperature, ultra-high-vacuum system capable of performing simultaneous STM imaging and 4-probe electrical transport characterization on the same sample.

**Method.** The setup integrates multiple techniques—SPM, UHV preparation tools (evaporator, cracker), and cryogenics—to allow *in situ* modification and measurement across wide temperature ranges.

**Model / system.** The platform is a specialized LT-UHV scanning probe microscope featuring two interconnected chambers. It supports measuring superconducting gaps via STS on materials like graphene and tracking the temperature dependence of resistivity/critical current in patterned nanowires (e.g., Pb).

**Key observables.** Local structural signatures (STM topography), spectroscopic features (superconducting gap $\Delta$), global electrical transport properties ($ho(T)$, $I_c(T)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature range: 2.9 K to 400 K; Mechanical stability: $<1$ pm; Spatial resolution: $5\ \mu	ext{m}$ landing accuracy in 10 minutes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system's complexity requires overcoming technical challenges related to integrating diverse capabilities (UHV, LT, SPM, transport) into one stable platform.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the complex UHV chamber design, the custom sample holder with six contacts for simultaneous measurement, and results showing superconducting transitions ($ho(T)$ dropping to zero) in Pb nanowires.

**Paper structure.** The paper describes the necessity of correlating local and global properties, details the engineering solution (the LT-UHV platform), demonstrates its capabilities through material preparation and precise positioning, and validates performance using measurements on graphene and superconducting nanowires.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We have developed a low temperature (LT), ultra-high-vacuum (UHV) system that combines two complementary techniques, scanning probe microscopy (SPM) and electrical transport measurements, within a single platform. By providing simultaneous access to the atomic-scale surface landscape and the macroscopic device response of the same sample, the setup enables direct correlations between local structural/spectroscopic signatures and global electronic transport behavior in two-dimensional (2D) devices. The system allows experiments where atomic-scale modifications or controlled manipulations are performed while continuously monitoring their impact on device-scale performance. The setup consists of two interconnected UHV chambers: a dedicated preparation chamber and a separate measurement chamber that houses a liquid-helium cryostat and the SPM/transport stage. Base pressure is 1x10^-11 Torr. A key feature is direct optical access to the sample, enabling rapid and reliable tip positioning with an accuracy of 5 microns x 5 microns within 10 minutes. The SPM, operated using custom-built electronics, can track the exact same sample region across a temperature range from 2.9 K to 400 K, with mechanical stability below 1 pm. System performance is demonstrated on graphene devices, and bulk Pb is used to determine energy resolution. Using superconducting tips, scanning tunneling spectroscopy measures the superconducting gap with an energy resolution of 30 microV. Transport measurements track the temperature dependence of both resistivity and critical current across the superconducting transition of an in-situ prepared Pb nanowire.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a detailed theoretical framework to analyze the reliability of work extraction from quantum Otto engines operating with spectrally constrained (homothetic) systems. By deriving exact formulas for work distributions and moments in the finite-size limit, it quantifies how deviations from ideal conditions—such as incomplete thermalization or spectral distortion—affect performance metrics like mean output versus statistical reliability.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous diagnostic tool to quantify non-ideal effects—like finite size or incomplete thermalization—in quantum energy conversion devices, which is crucial for designing practical quantum heat engines.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the finite-size reliability of work extraction from quantum heat engines, focusing on how fluctuations affect performance when energy spectra are discrete and finite.

**Main result.** It derives explicit formulas for the full work distribution and its moments for specific spectral structures, demonstrating that high-temperature/infinite-dimensional limits do not commute with finite-size effects.

**Method.** The analysis utilizes the Two-Point-Measurement (TPM) construction to calculate work statistics, specializing on homothetic spectra and extending the framework to include incomplete thermalization and finite driving times.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum Otto engine operating with 'homothetic' energy gaps, meaning all populated energy gaps are rescaled by a common factor. The system can be specialized to a uniformly spaced ladder connecting qubit and oscillator limits.

**Key observables.** Work distribution, mean work ($\langle W angle$), variance ($\sigma^2_W$), and signal-to-width reliability ($R_N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite number of energy levels ($N$), the scaling factor $\alpha$, temperature parameters ($eta_h, eta_l$), and spectral distortion measures.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that work statistics can be accurately derived using the Two-Point-Measurement (TPM) construction for energy-diagonal endpoint states; it also assumes quasistatic operation in the adiabatic limit.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the finite-$N$ crossover of reliability ($R_N$) for a uniform ladder, comparing exact results to infinite limits. Other figures compare mean work and reliability under sudden switches versus adiabatic driving.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using homothetic engines, derives moment equations via TPM, specializes these derivations for the uniformly spaced ladder case, compares different operational regimes (e.g., harmonic oscillator vs. ad-hoc), and finally analyzes deviations from ideal homothety.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Homothetic quantum Otto engines---where all populated energy gaps are rescaled by a common factor---provide a reference model in which the quasistatic stochastic efficiency is trajectory-independent while work remains fluctuating. For arbitrary finite homothetic spectra we derive the two-point-measurement work distribution and reduce the first two work moments to endpoint energy moments. Specializing to a uniformly spaced ladder gives closed finite-$N$ expressions for the full work distribution, mean work, variance, and signal-to-width reliability. This ladder connects the qubit and oscillator limits, reveals a finite-$N$ reliability crossover, and demonstrates that the high-temperature and infinite-dimensional limits do not commute. The noncommutation reflects a bounded-versus-unbounded spectral distinction: at fixed finite $N$ the Gibbs state has a normalizable infinite-temperature limit, whereas the oscillator retains an ever-expanding thermal tail. The exact formulas are used to compare standard mean-output prescriptions with work reliability, showing that maximum mean output and maximum dimensionless reliability select different operating points. The benchmark is extended to incomplete diagonal reset and to finite-time unitary strokes described by transition matrices, with a finite-ladder protocol and a harmonic sudden-switch oscillator benchmark as controlled examples. Weak deviations from exact homothety are treated perturbatively, showing how level-dependent gap distortions reintroduce quasistatic efficiency fluctuations and modify work reliability. Together, these results separate finite-size, incomplete thermalization, finite-time, and weak spectral-distortion contributions to work unreliability in quantum Otto engines.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a theoretical framework to dissect superselectivity in multivalent binding by decomposing the macroscopic response into contributions from receptor state reweighting and local occupation changes. It shows that this selectivity is fundamentally governed by how particle adsorption samples local receptor density fluctuations, providing measurable estimators for various physical regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous fluctuation-response framework applicable to adsorption phenomena, which shares mathematical structures with open quantum systems where environmental fluctuations dictate system behavior.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to microscopically understand the origin of 'superselectivity'—the sharp density discrimination observed during multivalent binding—by decomposing its macroscopic logarithmic response.

**Main result.** Superselectivity can be exactly decomposed into a receptor-state reweighting term and a direct occupation response, providing quantitative rules that link selectivity to local receptor fluctuations.

**Method.** The authors derive exact theoretical decompositions for the selectivity ($\alpha$) in terms of ensemble averages over occupied vs. random receptor counts, comparing results across various statistical limits.

**Model / system.** The system models multivalent particles adsorbing onto a surface with spatially fluctuating receptors, tested using lattice gas simulations and off-lattice hard-disk models.

**Key observables.** Superselectivity ($\alpha$), local receptor excess ($\langle n angle_{	ext{occ}} - ar{n}$), Fano factor ($F(ar{n})$), and the linear count mode weight ($M(ar{n})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Receptor statistics (Poisson, Binomial, Cox, Interacting LG), particle/receptor diameters ($d_R/\sigma$), and system density ($ar{n}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on deriving exact response decompositions; approximations are made when moving beyond the Poisson limit or dealing with finite-size effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare selectivity estimates (Finite Difference vs. Fano-rescaled vs. Count-resolved) across different receptor statistics and show how increasing receptor size ($d_R$) modifies these comparisons, especially at high coverage.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the general response decomposition theory, applies it to specific statistical ensembles (Poisson, Binomial, Cox), and then tests the robustness of the derived estimators using lattice/off-lattice simulations while analyzing finite size effects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superselective multivalent binding enables sharp receptor-density discrimination in targeting and sensing, but what its logarithmic response measures microscopically remains unclear. Here, we derive an exact response decomposition of the selectivity into receptor-state reweighting and a direct occupation response. In the dilute quenched Poisson limit, the direct term vanishes and selectivity is exactly the mean receptor excess beneath bound particles, which can be measured at a single density from a sufficiently large co-registered receptor-particle image. Beyond Poisson statistics, the excess is normalized by the Fano factor for a linear count response, while changes in distribution shape require the full count-resolved response. Lattice simulations verify these relations across distinct receptor statistics, while off-lattice simulations show that particle crowding enters through the direct term. These results establish a fluctuation-response framework linking multivalent selectivity to the local receptor fluctuations preferentially sampled by adsorption.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents Error-Generator-Level Compression, a novel Magnus-based control method designed to suppress complex coherent errors in fast quantum gates using minimal control parameters. By minimizing a penalty functional derived from the projected error generator, it achieves superior gate fidelity compared to existing techniques like DRAG. This approach is particularly valuable for superconducting qubits as it maintains physical insight into error sources while simplifying pulse design.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, physically motivated control technique that directly targets the structure of coherent errors in superconducting qubits, offering an alternative to standard unitary optimization methods by focusing on the underlying error generators.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is suppressing high-dimensional coherent error channels during fast quantum gate operations while ensuring the required control pulse remains simple to implement.

**Main result.** A novel 'Error-Generator-Level Compression' technique using a Magnus-based principle successfully suppresses errors by orders of magnitude, achieving target fidelity faster than established methods like DRAG.

**Method.** The authors introduce error-generator-level compression, which minimizes the projected error generator at a perturbative order while constraining the control waveform to only a few adjustable coefficients.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on fast quantum gates implemented on transmon qubits, modeled by a driven Hamiltonian that includes terms for target evolution and coherent errors. The analysis uses a four-level truncation of the system dynamics.

**Key observables.** Average fidelity error ($\epsilon$), projected error generator ($\hat{E}_{	ext{rel}}$), and residual eigenphases (diagnostics).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of adjustable pulse parameters (e.g., three for the compressed pulse vs. 17 for full correction) and the gate time scale ($|\alpha_2| t_f$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method does not make the truncated Magnus expansion exact; it selects a low-dimensional pulse by penalizing nonperturbatively large residual eigenphases.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare error suppression and fidelity performance across different control methods (compressed vs. DRAG) and demonstrate that the compressed pulse is less sensitive to finite-bandwidth filtering than fully parameterized pulses.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of high-dimensional error suppression, details the Magnus-based compression principle by minimizing a spectral penalty on the projected error generator, rigorously relates this functional minimization to the average fidelity error, and finally tests the robustness against realistic finite-bandwidth filtering effects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fast quantum gates in multilevel systems require suppressing many coherent-error channels using pulses that remain simple to implement. We introduce error-generator-level compression, a Magnus-based control principle that minimizes the complete projected error generator at a chosen perturbative order while restricting the implemented waveform to a few independently adjustable coefficients. This construction preserves all modeled computational and leakage errors rather than truncating the pulse by error channel, while its generator-level objective directly penalizes residual eigenphases that can signal departure from the perturbative regime. For fast transmon gates, a three-parameter pulse suppresses errors by orders of magnitude, outperforms grid-optimized leading- order DRAG over the gate-time range studied, and approaches a fully parameterized 17-parameter Magnus correction. The compressed pulse is also substantially less sensitive to the illustrative finite-bandwidth filtering model considered here.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the critical behavior of an extended XY spin chain with four-spin interactions by mapping it to a free fermion gas. It identifies Lifshitz-type topological quantum phase transitions characterized by band flattening at the critical point. This leads to unique, non-trivial power-law scaling for thermodynamic quantities like heat capacity and magnetic susceptibility near criticality.

**Why it may be interesting.** The derivation of singular power-law scaling laws ($T^{-1/3}$, $H^{-5/6}$) from a topological phase transition in a quantum spin model provides deep insights into critical phenomena beyond mean-field theory, relevant for understanding exotic magnetic materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates critical phases and quantum phase transitions (QPTs) within an extended isotropic XY chain incorporating a four-spin interaction.

**Main result.** The system exhibits Lifshitz-type topological QPTs at specific coupling ratios, leading to singular power-law behaviors in the density of states, heat capacity, and magnetic susceptibility near criticality.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Jordan-Wigner transformation to map the spin model onto an equivalent free spinless fermion (SF) gas, allowing for exact diagonalization techniques.

**Model / system.** The system is a spin S=1/2 isotropic XX chain with four-spin interactions. The Hamiltonian involves nearest-neighbor and next-next-next-neighbor couplings, which are mapped to hopping terms in the fermion representation.

**Key observables.** Ground state energy density, heat capacity (C), magnetic susceptibility ($\chi$), magnetization ($m$), and spin correlation functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $\alpha = J^*/J$ controls the phase transitions. Critical points are identified at $\alpha_c = 4/3$ for uniform fields and specific field strengths $H_c$ for alternating fields.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the spin system to a free fermion gas, which allows for exact solvability in terms of fermionic excitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dispersion relation showing changes in Fermi points across critical couplings ($\alpha$), and plot thermodynamic quantities like heat capacity ($C$) exhibiting non-linear behavior near the QCP.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the system's properties by first mapping the spin Hamiltonian to fermions, then calculating ground state energy and correlation functions as a function of coupling ratios ($\alpha$). It details distinct behaviors for uniform fields (Lifshitz transition) and alternating magnetic fields (AFM to SL transition).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using the Jordan-Wigner transformation we calculate exactly the ground state and low-temperature thermodynamic properties of the spin $S=1/2$ isotropic $XX$ chain with four spin interaction. In terms of the equivalent spinless fermion (SF) representation the system is viewed as a lattice fermion gas with nearest-neighbor ($J$) and next-next-next-neighbor ($J^{\ast}/4$) hopping. It is shown that with the increase of four spin coupling, at $J^{\ast}_{c} = 4J/3$ the system experiences the Lifshitz type topological phase transition characterized by the tripling of Fermi points. The quantum phase transition (QPT) point marks transition from a gapless spin-liquid phase of standard $XX$ chain into again a gapless spin-liquid phase with different character of power-low decay of spin correlations. At the transition point the free fermion dispersion relation shows flattering at Fermi points, what determines singular character of density of states $ρ(ω)\sim (ω/J)^{-2/3}$ and as a consequence unconventional temperature dependence of heat capacity of the system $C\sim (T/J^{\ast})^{1/3}$, and singular magnetic susceptibility of the system $χ(H)\sim (H/J^{\ast})^{-2/3}$.   In the case of alternating magnetic field the system is characterized by the rich ground state phase diagram which contains fully polarized (ferromagnetic), gapped antiferromagnetic (AFM) and spin liquid phases. At the transition point from the gapped AFM phase into the gapless polarized spin liquid phase the system shows rapid increase of magnetization $m\sim(H-H_c)^{1/6}$ and magnetic susceptibility a singular behavior as $χ(H)\sim (H-H_c)^{-5/6}$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper advances quantum error correction theory by proposing the 'moonwalking surface code' as an efficient alternative to traditional circuits. It achieves superior erasure error correction capacity using only simpler three-state readout instead of complex mid-circuit checks. The core technical contribution is a specialized branch-and-bound decoder designed to handle the non-trivial constraints arising from leakage errors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly detailed theoretical framework for fault-tolerant quantum computation, specifically addressing practical hardware limitations like mid-circuit measurement overheads in superconducting or trapped-ion architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing quantum error correction (QEC) circuits that achieve superior erasure error-correction capacity while minimizing hardware overhead associated with mid-circuit checks.

**Main result.** The authors show that the moonwalking surface code, when combined with three-state readout and assuming skip-gate leakage, can maintain erasure performance with zero overhead.

**Method.** They introduce a specialized branch-and-bound decoder to solve the quasi-Maximum Likelihood Estimation (quasi-MLE) problem for decoding errors under strict disjointness constraints.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the surface code, comparing static, walking, and moonwalking variants. The system models error channels including leakage, Pauli errors, and erasures, analyzed using three-state readout.

**Key observables.** Logical error rate scaling under erasure conditions; syndrome extraction circuits (Z stabilizer measurements); minimum weight perfect matching solutions ($ar{S}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The comparison is made across different leakage models (Depolarizing, Tailored, Skip-gate) and erasure check schedules.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include the faithfulness of three-state readout and modeling leaked qubits causing two-qubit gates to be entirely skipped (skip-gate model).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the gate sequences comparing walking vs. moonwalking circuits, showing how syndrome extraction is performed over multiple cycles. Other figures detail the structure of the branch-and-bound decoder algorithm flow.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically studies error correction protocols, introducing the moonwalking code as an improvement. It then details the necessary decoding machinery—the branch-and-bound algorithm—to handle the complex constraints imposed by leakage and erasure information.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error correction (QEC) codes can correct twice as many erasure errors as Pauli errors. Because of this scaling advantage, there is significant interest in developing qubits whose dominant error channel can be converted into erasures via mid-circuit erasure checks. However, such erasure checks come with hardware overhead in practice. End-of-the-line three-state readout, in which one simultaneously measures a qubit's erasure status and computational state, is an alternative to mid-circuit erasure checks that is generally simpler to implement. In this work, we systematically study the conditions required to enable erasure performance---the doubled error-correction capacity---in the surface code with and without mid-circuit erasure checks. We introduce the moonwalking surface code, the time-reversal of the walking surface code, as a zero-overhead circuit with superior handling of leakage and erasure. Specifically, we show that it enables erasure-like logical error rate scaling when combined with three-state measurement if leaked qubits cause two-qubit gates to be skipped and an appropriate decoder is used. Our decoder, based on a branch-and-bound algorithm, specifically incorporates the noise structure of the skip-gate leaked-qubit effect.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a fundamental criterion showing that Yang–Baxter integrability in quantum spin chains can be determined solely by checking a local conservation law called the Reshetikhin condition. This simplifies the search for integrable systems immensely by replacing the difficult task of finding R-matrices with a direct check on the Hamiltonian itself.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, experimentally accessible diagnostic tool for determining quantum integrability directly from the Hamiltonian, which is crucial for understanding complex many-body dynamics beyond simple models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is establishing a direct, Hamiltonian-level criterion for Yang–Baxter integrability, bypassing the traditional indirect method that starts from an R-matrix.

**Main result.** The authors prove that for translation-invariant spin chains, the Reshetikhin condition is necessary and sufficient for Yang–Baxter integrability, equating it to a local conservation law.

**Method.** The proof involves expanding the Yang–Baxter equation in spectral parameters and demonstrating that satisfying the lowest-order constraint (the Reshetikhin condition) forces all higher-order constraints to be satisfied.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quantum spin chains with nearest-neighbor interactions, governed by a local Hamiltonian $H = \sum h_{i, i+1}$. Integrability is characterized by the existence of an infinite hierarchy of local conserved quantities.

**Key observables.** Yang–Baxter integrability; the Reshetikhin condition (a specific commutator relation); and the conservation of the total energy current ($\mathcal{J}_E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spectral parameter $u$ and its relationship to local conserved charges $Q_n$. The system is assumed to be 1D with nearest-neighbor interactions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that the Reshetikhin condition implies the full Yang–Baxter equation under specific physical constraints (1D, translation-invariant).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 contrasts the conventional R-matrix $ightarrow$ Transfer Matrix approach with the proposed direct Hamiltonian search. Figure 2 relates integrability to energy current conservation, and Figure 3 illustrates how the Reshetikhin condition propagates through the YBE expansion.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from defining the problem (Hamiltonian vs. R-matrix) to introducing the necessary and sufficient criterion (Reshetikhin condition). It then provides detailed mathematical proofs involving spectral parameter expansions, culminating in showing that this low-order test is equivalent to the full algebraic structure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum integrability is a cornerstone of the exact theory of interacting quantum spin chains. In its standard formulation, however, one starts from R-matrices satisfying the Yang--Baxter equation, rather than from the Hamiltonian itself. It has therefore remained unclear how Yang--Baxter solvability can be characterized directly at the Hamiltonian level, and how it is related to the existence of local conservation laws. Here we prove that, in a broad standard setting, the Reshetikhin condition is not only necessary but also sufficient for Yang--Baxter integrability, thereby reducing the hidden algebraic structure of integrability to a Hamiltonian-level conservation law. Since the Reshetikhin condition is equivalent to conservation of the total energy current, this Hamiltonian-level criterion is also experimentally accessible. This result establishes a quantum counterpart of the Liouville--Arnold theorem for isotropic spin chains, stating that Yang--Baxter solvability is equivalent to an infinite hierarchy of local conserved quantities. Our result also simplifies substantially the search for integrable spin chains by replacing the search for R-matrices with a direct criterion on local Hamiltonians.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.29623v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical study examines how quantum confinement interacts with the elasticity of helical nanoribbons. By calculating a total geometric potential, the authors show that injecting an electron stabilizes the ribbon into a specific 'normal' conformation. This suggests that electronic interactions can drive structural changes in curved nanomaterials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects geometry (curvature) directly to electronic properties (potential minima), offering a mechanism for emergent phenomena like Hall effects in nanoscale structures without external fields.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how placing a quantum particle on an elastic helical nanoribbon influences its conformational stability, leading to potential transitions.

**Main result.** Electron injection induces a conformational transition towards the normal ribbon conformation because this state yields the lowest total geometric potential. This process suggests a mechanism for generating a Hall-like voltage purely from curvature effects.

**Method.** The authors derived a total potential by summing an elastic potential (from a modified Canham-Helfrich model) and a quantum geometric potential (from da Costa's formulation). They analyzed the resulting 1D Schrödinger equation by varying dimensionless parameters.

**Model / system.** The system is an elastic helical nanoribbon whose conformation is parameterized. The physics is governed by a total potential $U_{total} = U_Q + U_{elastic}$, which dictates the particle's localized states and the ribbon's preferred geometry.

**Key observables.** Total geometric potential, existence/location of localized quantum states, and the conformational parameter $\zeta_0$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionless ratio $R_H$ (comparing quantum to elastic energy scales) is crucial; critical values of $R_H$ dictate when localization occurs.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ribbon is elastic and uses approximations like $D_1 \approx D_2$ for simplifying the elastic energy model.

**Figures summary.** Figures plot the total potential $U(\xi)$ versus position $\xi$ for various values of $R_H$, demonstrating how the minimum potential shifts from the binormal to the normal conformation as $R_H$ increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the elastic and quantum potentials based on curvature ($M, K$), combining them into a total potential, and then analyzing this potential landscape by varying the dimensionless ratio $R_H$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider an {\em elastic} helical nanoribbon that can take on various conformations, and study the effect of placing a quantum particle on its curved surface. Using a modified Canham-Helfrich model for the elastic energy, we write down the local elastic potential for the ribbon in terms of its bending rigidity, mean curvature $M$ and Gaussian curvature $K$. The Schrödinger equation of a particle confined to a {\em rigid} curved surface is found using da Costa's formulation. It has a purely quantum geometric potential which depends on $M$ and $K$. The Schrödinger equation of a particle on an {\em elastic } curved surface will therefore have a total potential comprising quantum and elastic potentials. We compute $M$ and $K$ for a helical ribbon and derive the total potential which depends on the conformation and is thus geometric in nature. Defining a dimensionless quantity $R_H$, we study the behavior of the total geometric potential as $R_H$ is varied. In the absence of an electron, the elastic potential is positive and has a single positive maximum for all conformations. Further, a binormal helical ribbon conformation has the lowest potential, while the normal ribbon has the highest, with those of the intermediate ribbons lying in between these. Intriguingly, when a quantum particle is placed on the elastic ribbon, above a certain critical value of $R_H$, the presence of the quantum geometric potential {\em reverses} this order. But localized states for the particle are not supported. Only above a second critical value of $R_H$, localized states appear for all conformations. The injection of an electron on {\it any} given conformation of the elastic ribbon will induce a conformational transition to the normal ribbon conformation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the global quantum Fisher information matrix (g-QFIM) as the fundamental constraint governing the total information capacity of quantum systems performing multiple tasks simultaneously. It derives rigorous upper bounds on the multi-task Holevo information, revealing that performance is governed by an underlying information geometry rather than simple summation. This provides crucial theoretical guidance for designing next-generation quantum networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, geometric framework for quantifying multi-task capabilities in quantum networks, moving beyond simple additive capacity models and offering design principles for integrated systems like ISAC.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining the fundamental, unified limit on the total information capacity of a single quantum carrier when multiple distinct tasks compete for its resources.

**Main result.** The total task capacity is fundamentally constrained by an underlying global information geometry captured by the global quantum Fisher information matrix (g-QFIM).

**Method.** The authors derive non-asymptotic, measurement-independent upper bounds on the Holevo information using information geometric principles and bounding techniques like Jensen's inequality.

**Model / system.** The analysis considers general multi-task quantum systems, exemplified by a minimal model using a two-mode quantum carrier encoded in phases (e.g., photonic phase encoding).

**Key observables.** Holevo information capacity ($\chi$), Global Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (g-QFIM) $A$, and task-directional Fisher lengths ($L_i$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of tasks ($K$), the physical resource level (e.g., mean excitation $N$), and noise parameters (attenuation, phase diffusion).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derived bounds rely on specific mathematical inequalities (Jensen's, Cauchy–Schwarz) and assume certain factorizations for prior distributions in the general K-task case.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the common information acquisition structure across tasks and compare theoretical capacity bounds ($	ext{Tr} \mathbf{A}$) against actual Holevo information as physical resources or noise levels change, highlighting an optimal operating point.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by identifying the need for a unified metric. It then derives two key theorems bounding the joint Holevo information using local distinguishability ($L_i$) and the global g-QFIM ($	ext{Tr} A$). The analysis proceeds to explore structural implications, such as phase transitions in performance based on resource allocation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Future quantum networks are expected to perform multiple tasks simultaneously within a single system, such as integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) architectures. Despite various metrics, we find that the evaluation of multiple tasks can be unified by their information capacity, and the total task capacity is not determined simply by addition, but is fundamentally constrained by an information geometry which we call the global quantum Fisher information matrix (g-QFIM). With this insight, we derive a non-asymptotic, measurement-independent upper bound on the Holevo information for multi-task systems, which takes a Shannon-capacity-like form. It not only quantifies the capacity limit, but also the allocability. Our results reveal a structural phase transition in multi-task performance under resource variation, where additional physical resources no longer increase independent task capacity but instead concentrate more information into a new mono-task mode. Numerical simulations based on photonic phase encoding and realistic noise channels confirm these predictions. This work establishes a unified information-geometric principle for quantum multi-task systems, with implications for the design of future quantum networks and ISAC architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how different theoretical null models affect the statistical validation of projected bipartite networks. The authors demonstrate that simply matching structural constraints is insufficient; instead, one must analyze the resulting probability distribution's moments (mean and variance). They conclude that the statistical consequences induced by a model's assumptions are the true measure for comparing these random ensembles.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about quantum systems, the rigorous statistical framework developed for comparing null models based on induced probability distributions is highly relevant to analyzing complex correlation structures found in quantum many-body simulations or network representations derived from quantum information processes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The statistical validation of projected bipartite networks requires comparing observed co-occurrences against random expectations derived from a null model, making the choice of this null model critical.

**Main result.** Null models should be compared based on the statistical consequences their constraints induce on the test statistic's probability distribution, rather than solely on the structural constraints they preserve.

**Method.** The authors compare four null models (Microcanonical, BiCM, BiPCM, Hypergeometric) by analyzing the first two moments (expectation and variance) of the co-occurrence random variable across three empirical datasets.

**Model / system.** The system involves projected bipartite networks derived from real-world data like comparative genomics or international trade. The analysis focuses on the co-occurrence random variable, $X_{jj'}$, which measures shared neighbors between two nodes in one set based on connections through a second set.

**Key observables.** Co-occurrence random variable ($X_{jj'}$), statistical p-value, expected value ($\langle X_{jj'} angle$), and variance ($	ext{Var}(X_{jj'})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Degree heterogeneity of the non-projected layer; network size (sparse limit analysis); validation metrics like F1 score.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison relies on analyzing the first two moments (expectation and variance) to understand differences, even when full probability distributions are intractable. The Hypergeometric model surprisingly captures the microcanonical variance despite neglecting heterogeneity.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 shows the ratio of approximated vs BiCM expected co-occurrence for three datasets; Table 4 reports validation statistics (F1 score, Recall) comparing different null models against a microcanonical benchmark.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of statistical validation in projected bipartite networks. It details four competing null models and compares them using empirical data from genomics, trade, and food science. The core argument develops that distributional properties (moments) are more important than structural constraints alone, culminating in a comparison of model performance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Statistical validation of projected bipartite networks depends critically on the null model adopted to describe random co-occurrences. Although several null models have been proposed, their comparison has mainly focused on the validated backbones they produce rather than on the statistical assumptions underlying their construction. Here we compare four widely used null models - the microcanonical configuration model generated by the Curveball algorithm, the Bipartite Configuration Model (BiCM), the Bipartite Partial Configuration Model (BiPCM), and the Hypergeometric approximation - using three empirical bipartite systems from comparative genomics, international trade and food science. We use the statistically validated links obtained from the microcanonical bipartite configuration model as the reference benchmark for assessing performances of the other three models. Our central result is that the statistical consequences of relaxing null-model constraints cannot be understood solely from the constraints themselves but must be analyzed through the probability distribution induced for the co-occurrence statistic. In particular, the combined behaviour of the expectation and variance largely explains the observed differences among the statistically validated backbones. We further derive a leading-order sparse approximation for the BiCM expectation, showing that the first correction to the Hypergeometric prediction is controlled by the degree heterogeneity of the non-projected layer. Surprisingly, despite neglecting this heterogeneity, the Hypergeometric model accurately reproduces the co-occurrence variance of the microcanonical ensemble across all datasets. Our results suggest that null models should be compared not only according to the constraints they preserve but also according to the statistical consequences that these constraints induce on the distribution of the test statistic.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper details a method to generate and store high levels of nuclear spin squeezing ($	ext{SSS}$) in noble gases by coupling them to alkali-metal spins via controlled spin-exchange collisions. By applying a specific magnetic field, the authors overcome limitations related to atomic number mismatch, enabling the transfer of quantum correlations into macroscopic ensembles for long-term storage and subsequent measurement.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it proposes a robust, scalable platform for quantum memory utilizing long-lived nuclear spins. The ability to engineer coherence transfer between different types of spins using external fields is a key technique in open quantum systems and quantum sensing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is efficiently generating, manipulating, and measuring nuclear spin squeezing (SSS) in noble-gas systems that possess ultra-long coherence times but are difficult to manipulate.

**Main result.** The authors propose using coherent spin-exchange collisions with alkali-metal ensembles, controlled by an external magnetic field, to transfer SSS to macroscopic noble-gas spins ($\ge 10^{20}$ atoms) and store it for long periods.

**Method.** The method involves applying a specific resonant magnetic field ($B_r$) to mediate the spin exchange, enabling controllable transfer of quantum correlations from one spin ensemble to another.

**Model / system.** The system consists of two coupled spin ensembles: alkali-metal electronic spins and noble-gas nuclear spins. The interaction is modeled by a Hamiltonian derived from coherent spin-exchange collisions, which can be tuned using an external magnetic field $B$.

**Key observables.** Spin-squeezing parameter ($\xi^2$), evolution of squeezing parameters $\xi_s^2(t)$ and $\xi_i^2(t)$, and the variance of spin components.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strength ($J$), atomic number ratio ($r = n_i/n_s$), and the applied magnetic field $B$ (especially the resonant field $B_r$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The process relies on achieving a strong-coupling regime ($J \gg \Gamma_s$) for efficient transfer, and assumes that the noble-gas spins retain coherence over days.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic setup (alkali/noble gas mixture), the three-step cycle of squeezing manipulation (Generation $ightarrow$ Storage $ightarrow$ Measurement) using magnetic field control, and parameter evolution vs. field deviation.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by first establishing the physical model via the spin-exchange Hamiltonian, then deriving the conditions for resonant transfer using a specific magnetic field $B_r$, and finally detailing the three-step protocol (preparation, storage, readout) necessary to demonstrate measurable SSS in the noble gas.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Isolation from environment leads to the days-long lifetime of noble-gas nuclear spins, but also brings great challenges to the preparation, manipulation, and measurement of nuclear-spin quantum states. Here we find that nuclear spin squeezing, with ultra-long lifetime and huge atomic number, can be efficiently obtained and manipulated based on the coherent spin-exchange interaction between alkali-metal and noble-gas ensembles. Thanks to the considerable advantage of our proposal in preparing the spin squeezing of the macroscopic atomic ensemble with a huge atomic number, even the nuclear spin-squeezed state containing 10^20 atoms or more is obtainable with preexisting techniques. Further, the days-long storage and measurement of nuclear spin squeezing can be performed by the coherent manipulation of a magnetic field. This proposal can be implemented in hot atomic ensembles, whose ease of access and high adaptability to various environments will significantly facilitate the research and application of nuclear-spin nonclassical states in precision measurement, quantum information, and fundamental physics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes sound attenuation and velocity shifts in spin-1/2 antiferromagnetic chains. It connects these measurable elastic responses to the fundamental thermodynamic derivatives of the system's free energy. By combining bosonization with advanced quantum statistical mechanics, it predicts universal behaviors near critical points and provides quantitative scaling laws for various temperature regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical link between macroscopic elastic properties (sound velocity) and microscopic quantum magnetic interactions (spin Hamiltonian parameters), which is highly relevant to understanding emergent phenomena in correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how sound attenuation and velocity shifts (magnetoelastic coupling) manifest in antiferromagnetic spin-1/2 chains subjected to an external magnetic field.

**Main result.** The analysis predicts universal behavior near quantum critical points, such as $z=2$, and establishes relationships between the elastic response and derivatives of the system's free energy with respect to exchange interactions.

**Method.** The work employs a combination of linear response theory, bosonization techniques for low-energy physics in the Tomonaga-Luttinger liquid phase, and advanced thermodynamic calculations using quantum transfer matrix methods.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is an antiferromagnetic spin-1/2 XXZ chain coupled to longitudinal acoustic phonons. The Hamiltonian includes terms describing the spin interactions ($H_0$) and the magnetoelastic coupling ($H_{ap}, H_1, H_2$).

**Key observables.** Sound velocity shift ($\Delta\omega_k$), ultrasound attenuation coefficient ($\alpha_k$), and derivatives of the free energy ($F$) with respect to exchange couplings.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), external magnetic field ($h$), and coupling constants like $J$ and $J_z$. Key regimes include the Tomonaga-Luttinger liquid phase and the fully polarized phase.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on perturbative expansions for response functions, using bosonization for low-energy limits, and relating elastic properties to thermodynamic derivatives of the free energy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how the first and second derivatives of the free energy ($\partial F / \partial J$ and $\partial^2 F / \partial J^2$) exhibit cusp-like singularities or divergences near magnetic saturation ($h = \pm J$), leading to predicted dips in sound velocity.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from general linear response theory for the spin-phonon coupling, moves to specialized low-energy treatments using bosonization (TLL phase), and culminates in calculating thermodynamic derivatives of the free energy via quantum transfer matrix methods for finite temperatures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate ultrasound attenuation and sound velocity shift in antiferromagnetic spin-1/2 XXZ chains in magnetic field. We relate the sound velocity shift to derivatives of the free energy with respect to exchange interactions, permitting its calculation with integrability techniques at any temperature. Using bosonization, we predict the sound velocity shift exhibits a quadratic temperature correction at low temperatures in the Tomonaga-Luttinger liquid phase. Close to the fully polarized phase, a universal behavior associated with z=2 quantum criticality is found. In the Tomonaga-Luttinger liquid phase, ultrasound attenuation obeys a scaling law as a function of wavelength and temperature. An enhancement of attenuation is obtained near the fully polarized phase.

</details>
