**Summary.** This theoretical study uses an advanced DFPT+U method to examine how strain modifies the electronic and optical properties of ZnO. The research finds that moderate tensile strain can selectively boost electron mobility by 19% without compromising the material's transparency in the visible spectrum. This establishes a powerful computational tool for designing high-performance, strain-engineered oxide semiconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the use of advanced correlated electron methods like DFT+U to model material responses under external perturbations (strain) is relevant for understanding many-body effects in quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how strain affects key performance parameters like carrier mobility and optical absorption in oxide semiconductors for use in transparent displays.

**Main result.** Uniaxial tensile strain up to 4.8% along [\bar{1}10] increases room-temperature electron mobility by 19% while keeping visible-range optical absorption essentially unchanged.

**Method.** The authors employ a newly developed Density-Functional Perturbation Theory with a self-consistent Hubbard correction (DFPT+U) to model electron-phonon interactions and correlation effects.

**Model / system.** The system studied is strained Zinc Oxide (ZnO), modeled using first-principles calculations that account for on-site Coulomb repulsion via the Hubbard U term, particularly applied to the O 2p manifold.

**Key observables.** Room-temperature electron mobility, optical absorption spectrum, lattice parameters ($a', c'$), and band gap energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Uniaxial tensile strain ($\varepsilon_{yy}$) up to 4.8%; Hubbard parameter $U_p$; Temperature (300 K).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the minimal Hubbard correction applied only to the O 2p orbital is sufficient, and it focuses on in-plane tensile strain relevant for thin films.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate atomic structures under strain, electronic/phonon band dispersions, temperature dependence of mobility, and changes in optical absorption spectra as a function of strain.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the theoretical framework (DFPT+U), calculating structural relaxations and electronic properties under varying uniaxial strains, quantifying the resulting changes in carrier transport (mobility) and optical response (absorption).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Carrier mobility and optical absorption are key performance parameters of oxide semiconductors in transparent and flexible displays. We use a newly developed density-functional perturbation theory with a self-consistent Hubbard correction (DFPT+U) to study phonon-limited electron transport and phonon-assisted optical absorption in strained zinc oxide (ZnO). This parameter-free approach accounts for electron-phonon interactions and on-site correlation effects simultaneously. Electronic structures and phonon dispersions are computed under three distinct uniaxial strain directions. Uniaxial tensile strain up to 4.8% along [\bar110] is found to increase the room-temperature electron mobility by 19% while leaving visible-range optical absorption essentially unchanged. These results demonstrate that moderate strain can selectively enhance carrier transport without degrading optical transparency, and establish DFPT+U as an effective framework for predicting strain-dependent transport and optical properties in wide-band-gap oxides with implications for strain-engineered display and optoelectronic applications.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes an advanced method for training quantum generative models based on IQP circuits by incorporating classical physics knowledge. It uses a Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) to generate a structured latent code that initializes the quantum model's parameters. This physics-informed initialization significantly improves the model's ability to accurately and efficiently generate solutions to parameterized PDEs compared to purely random initialization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum machine learning with continuum physics modeling by using deep learning techniques (PINNs) to inject physical constraints into the initial state of a quantum model, which is highly relevant for simulating complex quantum many-body systems where accurate initialization is crucial.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to improve quantum generative learning based on IQP circuits by replacing random latent variable initialization with a physics-informed scheme.

**Main result.** Physics-informed initialization derived from a PINN surrogate consistently outperforms random initialization, leading to improved adaptation behavior and stronger generative accuracy for solving PDEs like Burgers' equation.

**Method.** The authors train a Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) on low-resolution PDE solutions to extract a latent representation, which is then used to initialize the latent variables of an IQP quantum model for subsequent high-resolution adaptation.

**Model / system.** The system involves solving parameterized Partial Differential Equations (PDEs), specifically Burgers' equation. The modeling framework combines an IQP circuit (quantum) with a PINN surrogate (classical/ML) to learn the solution manifold in a latent space.

**Key observables.** Reconstruction accuracy, Mean Squared Error (MSE) of the generated PDE solutions across varying viscosity settings ($
u$), and pairwise latent correlations between different physical parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Viscosity parameter ($
u$, e.g., $\alpha$ or $
u \in [0.11, 0.51]$), low-dimensional latent vector size ($d_{	ext{lat}}=50$), and the structure of the IQP circuit (e.g., 18 qubits).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach relies on a Representation Hypothesis suggesting that classical and quantum models parameterize the same underlying PDE solution manifold, allowing for latent space transfer.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the three-stage process: (a) PINN training on low-res data; (b) transferring the classical latent $z(
u)$ to initialize the IQP latent variable $	heta_{	ext{lat}}$; and (c) two-stage IQP adaptation on high-resolution data.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of inefficient random initialization in quantum generative learning. It proposes using a PINN surrogate trained on PDEs to generate a physics-informed latent code, which initializes the IQP model. The methodology details the two-stage training process (shared core $ightarrow$ latent adaptation) and validates this against random initialization and classical baselines like cDCGANs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum generative learning based on instantaneous quantum polynomial-time (IQP) circuits can benefit from efficient classical training strategies. A recent latent adaptation framework for IQP-based generative modeling transfers shared circuit parameters across instances of the same task with different hyperparameters while adapting only a low-dimensional latent variable for each new instance. However, existing approaches initialize this latent variable randomly, which can limit optimization efficiency and performance. In this work, we introduce a physics-informed latent initialization scheme for IQP generative learning to improve upon existing random initialization schemes. Motivated by the platonic representation hypothesis, we use latent representations extracted from a classical physics-informed neural network (PINN) surrogate to initialize the latent variables of the quantum model for the solution of the Burgers' equation. The initialized IQP model is then adapted on a higher-resolution solution domain. We find that this structured initialization consistently outperforms random latent initialization, yielding improved adaptation behavior and stronger generative accuracy across multiple viscosity settings. These results show that classical surrogate representations can provide useful inductive bias for quantum generative models and offer a practical route to improved initialization in IQP-based learning.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.29249v1>

**Summary.** This work develops and validates machine learning models to predict key magnetic properties of hard magnets by analyzing data from extensive micromagnetic simulations. By combining ML with symbolic regression, the authors achieve high accuracy while drastically accelerating the prediction process compared to traditional physics simulations. This provides a powerful tool for materials discovery in magnetism.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical magnetism, the methodology—using advanced machine learning surrogates to bypass computationally expensive physics simulations—is highly relevant to modeling complex quantum many-body systems where direct simulation is intractable.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is predicting extrinsic magnetic properties (coercive field, remanence, maximum energy product) of hard magnets from their intrinsic micromagnetic parameters.

**Main result.** Machine learning models and symbolic regression significantly outperform established analytical models in predicting these properties with lower errors. The developed pipeline allows for rapid screening of candidate material parameters.

**Method.** The study employs machine learning (Random Forest, Gaussian Process) trained on extensive micromagnetic simulations, supplemented by symbolic regression to derive closed-form physical laws.

**Model / system.** The system is an idealized isolated cubic grain used to model magnetization reversal in hard magnets. The physics incorporates isotropic exchange, uniaxial anisotropy, and demagnetizing interactions within a micromagnetic framework.

**Key observables.** Coercive field (Hc), remanent magnetisation (Mr), maximum energy product (BHmax).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Saturation magnetisation (Ms), exchange constant (A), uniaxial anisotropy constant (K); the magnetic hardness parameter $\kappa = K / (\mu_0 M_s^2)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on micromagnetic simulations of an ideal, defect-free cubic grain and assumes that ML models can accurately map complex physical relationships.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between ML predictions and true values for intrinsic parameters (Ms, A, K) in the hard-magnet subset. Tables compare model errors (MAPE/RMSE) when performing the inverse problem (predicting intrinsic from extrinsic).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the prediction problem, details data generation via 12012 micromagnetic simulations across a broad parameter space, compares ML and symbolic regression against analytical models for all three properties, investigates the inverse problem, and concludes by releasing a fast predictive pipeline.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Predicting the extrinsic properties from hysteresis loops of a magnetic grain, namely the coercive field, remanent magnetisation, and maximum energy product, from its intrinsic micromagnetic parameters is a central problem in permanent-magnet modelling. Established analytical models provide useful estimates but often neglect nonuniform magnetisation processes, whereas direct micromagnetic simulations are computationally expensive. In this work, we train machine-learning models on 12012 micromagnetic simulations of an idealised cubic grain, spanning broad ranges of the saturation magnetisation, exchange constant, and uniaxial anisotropy constant. Benchmarked against the analytical models on identical held-out data, the machine-learning models predict all three extrinsic properties with substantially lower errors. Symbolic regression recovers the Kronmüller form of the coercive field, with an effective demagnetising factor that depends on the material, and finds new closed-form expressions for the remanence and maximum energy product. Each law contains at most two fitted constants yet approaches the accuracy of the machine-learning models. We also investigate the inverse problem of recovering the intrinsic parameters from the three extrinsic properties. The saturation magnetisation and anisotropy constant are recovered accurately, whereas the exchange constant is not, because it influences the extrinsic properties only weakly. The trained models are released through the mammos-ai Python package, enabling thousands of candidate parameter sets to be screened in seconds rather than the hours or days required by direct micromagnetic simulation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the angular localization of quantum states on curved surfaces subjected to random potentials. The core finding is that the characteristic length scale at which the system transitions from being governed by radial confinement to angular confinement depends critically on the manifold's curvature. This leads to a distinct logarithmic scaling law on hyperbolic planes compared to flat space.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous theoretical framework for understanding how geometric constraints (curvature) fundamentally alter localization physics in disordered quantum systems, which has implications for wave propagation in curved media or complex lattice structures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how angular localization of radially localized quantum states is amplified by the curvature of the underlying manifold, distinguishing this effect from generic metal-insulator transitions.

**Main result.** The crossover radius at which shell breaking occurs exhibits a distinct functional dependence on curvature: algebraic in flat space but logarithmic ($\propto 2a\ln(1/\varepsilon)$) on hyperbolic surfaces.

**Method.** The authors employ exact angular-momentum decomposition, transfer-matrix calculations, and direct polar-grid simulations to analyze the localization length in both radial and angular directions.

**Model / system.** The system is a continuum Schrödinger particle moving on a 2D Riemannian surface of constant curvature $K$, subjected to a dominant radial random potential ($V_r$) and a weaker, angle-dependent perturbation ($\varepsilon V_\perp$).

**Key observables.** Radial Lyapunov exponent ($\gamma_r$), angular localization length ($\xi_	heta$), shell-breaking radius ($r^*$), and the single-state normalized shell-extension indicator ($Q_{sh}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gaussian curvature $K$, curvature radius $a$, radial disorder width $W_r$, and angular perturbation amplitude $\varepsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on separating genuine asymptotic localization from finite-size crossovers, and the derived crossover law is specific to this deliberately anisotropic disorder ensemble.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the geometry and localization mechanisms. Figure 2(a) compares Born approximation and transfer-matrix results for $\gamma_r$. Figure 9 plots the weak-disorder coefficient vs. $\ln(1/\epsilon)$ and derives the crossover radius $r^*/a$ based on curvature.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing radial localization via Lyapunov exponents in the symmetry limit ($\varepsilon=0$), to analyzing the effective disordered ring Hamiltonian, and finally deriving the curvature-dependent crossover law by comparing flat space, hyperbolic, and general constant-curvature regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a continuum Schrödinger particle on a surface of constant curvature subject to a radial random potential and a weaker angle-dependent perturbation. Exact angular-momentum decomposition reduces the rotationally symmetric problem to independent one-dimensional random radial channels. A positive radial Lyapunov exponent distinguishes radial Anderson localization from the circular probability profile imposed by symmetry. Projection of a narrow radial mode produces a disordered one-dimensional ring. For angular disorder that is stationary in physical arc length, the ring localization length grows as the inverse square of the perturbation amplitude. Equating this length with the geodesic circumference yields a shell-breaking radius that grows algebraically in flat space but as $2a\ln(1/\varepsilon)+O(1)$ on a hyperbolic surface of curvature radius $a$ and angular-disorder amplitude $\varepsilon$. Transfer-matrix and finite-ring calculations determine the crossover coefficient. Direct polar-grid calculations at the same angular energy as the ring model use unbiased tracking of the degenerate angular-momentum subspace across six independent radial-disorder realizations and quantify the validity of the single-mode projection. The noncommuting weak-disorder and flat-curvature limits establish a curvature-amplified crossover for this deliberately anisotropic disorder ensemble, rather than a generic metal--insulator transition on the hyperbolic plane.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically investigates the formation of hybrid electronic networks in twisted rhombohedral graphene by analyzing the coexistence of localized and propagating 1D electronic states. Using an effective Hamiltonian derived from moiré potentials, the authors demonstrate that this system naturally supports distinct types of electronic behavior across its lattice structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** The emergence of distinct electronic dimensionalities (localized vs. quasi-1D) within a single moiré lattice structure provides a rich playground for studying correlated electron physics, potentially leading to novel topological or superconducting phases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the coexistence of localized electronic states and propagating one-dimensional (1D) modes within moiré graphene systems.

**Main result.** The authors establish twisted monolayer–rhombohedral graphene as a promising platform for realizing hybrid electronic networks exhibiting coexisting states of distinct effective dimensionalities.

**Method.** The analysis uses continuum models and perturbation theory to derive an effective low-energy Hamiltonian acting on the monolayer graphene, incorporating spatially varying potentials derived from moiré patterns.

**Model / system.** The physical system is twisted monolayer–rhombohedral N-layer graphene. The model involves deriving an effective Moiré Hamiltonian by integrating out the gapped rhombohedral substrate layer, resulting in terms dependent on a scalar potential ($V_0$) and a staggered potential ($V_s$).

**Key observables.** Band structures (showing 1D bands and flat bands), constant-energy contours, real-space probability distributions of Bloch wave functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative twist angle ($	heta$), the energy regime within the gap ($U_{N+1} < E < U_2$), and characteristic length scales ($\xi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key approximations include using a minimal parametrization for potentials, neglecting lattice-relaxation-induced corrugation, and employing the local approximation in the effective Hamiltonian derivation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the moiré pattern, spatial profiles of $V_s(\mathbf{r})$ (showing sign changes) and $V_0(\mathbf{r})$ (showing minima), band structures showing both 1D and flat bands, and constant-energy contours confirming quasi-1D propagation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from a minimal toy model analysis demonstrating the coexistence of localized states (from $V_0$) and 1D modes (from sign changes in $V_s$), to a full microscopic realization using twisted multilayer graphene, where the effective Hamiltonian is derived via perturbation theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the coexistence of localized states and propagating one-dimensional (1D) modes in graphene moiré systems. We first show within a minimal model that a spatially varying scalar potential can confine localized states, while sign changes of a staggered potential generate 1D modes along the resulting domain walls. These two types of states can coexist within the same finite energy window and form a hybrid network. We then demonstrate a microscopic realization of this mechanism in twisted monolayer-rhombohedral N-layer graphene. Band structures, energy contours, and Bloch wave functions obtained in a realistic parameter regime reveal the coexistence of localized nearly flat-band states and propagating quasi-1D modes. Our results establish twisted monolayer-rhombohedral graphene as a promising platform for realizing hybrid electronic networks with coexisting states of distinct effective dimensionalities.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.28983v1>

**Summary.** The paper develops a generalized Holstein-Primakoff transformation that offers a flexible way to optimize bosonic truncations in spin-wave theory. By tuning an interpolation parameter, the authors significantly reduce approximation errors when studying models like the BBH Hamiltonian on a square lattice. The optimized results show excellent quantitative agreement with advanced tensor-network calculations for key magnetic excitations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a systematic, tunable theoretical tool (the generalized HP transformation) to improve quantitative accuracy in strongly correlated magnetic systems by optimizing the underlying mathematical representation used in mean-field approximations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Spin-wave theory requires bosonizing spin operators, but the resulting Hamiltonian must be truncated, leading to results dependent on the chosen bosonic representation.

**Main result.** A generalized Holstein-Primakoff transformation allows continuous optimization of the bosonic truncation parameter, substantially reducing errors and yielding quantitative agreement with tensor-network calculations for excitation spectra.

**Method.** The authors introduce a generalized HP transformation parameterized by $x$ that interpolates between known transformations. They optimize this representation by minimizing the ground-state energy or matching spectral features against highly accurate tensor-network benchmarks.

**Model / system.** The primary application is to the spin-1 bilinear-biquadratic (BBH) model on a square lattice, particularly in the ferroquadrupolar phase. The theory extends to $	ext{SU}(N)$ algebras and utilizes automatic differentiation for implementation.

**Key observables.** Excitation spectra (quasiparticle dispersions), dynamical spin structure factors ($\mathcal{A}_{k\omega}$), and ground-state energy ($E_g$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interpolation parameter $x$ ($0 \le x \le 1$), the coupling constants defining the BBH model, and the lattice momentum $\mathbf{k}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The accuracy of spin-wave theory is limited by the finite order of truncation; however, optimizing the representation mitigates this dependence.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare quadrupolar mode dispersion ($\omega$ vs $k$) and spectral functions between optimized Continuous Similarity Transformation (CST) results and Tensor Network (TN) calculations, showing excellent quantitative agreement, especially reproducing interaction-induced roton minima.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the generalized HP transformation and its extension to $	ext{SU}(N)$. It then applies this framework to the spin-1 BBH model on a square lattice, comparing results derived from optimized GSWT against established TN benchmarks for excitation spectra and spectral functions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin-wave theory provides a quasiparticle description of excitation spectra in quantum spin systems. In this theory, the spin operators are usually bosonized by the Holstein-Primakoff transformation or the Dyson-Maleev transformation. In practical calculations, the bosonized Hamiltonian has to be truncated, and thus the resulting excitation spectra depend on the choice of bosonic representation. Here, we introduce a generalized Holstein-Primakoff transformation that continuously interpolates between the conventional Holstein-Primakoff and Dyson-Maleev transformations through a single parameter. The formulation naturally extends to the SU(N) algebra and provides a flexible framework for optimizing bosonic truncations beyond harmonic order. As an application, we investigate the spin-1 bilinear-biquadratic model on the square lattice. By combining the generalized Holstein-Primakoff transformation with continuous similarity transformations, we obtain excitation spectra in excellent agreement with tensor-network calculations. Our results demonstrate that optimizing the bosonic representation substantially reduces truncation errors and provides quantitatively reliable descriptions of both quasiparticle dispersions and multi-boson continua.

</details>

The solid blue line represents the exact classical solution derived via standard matrix diagonalization, while the red markers indicate the eigenvalues computed using the Quantum RVVQE algorithm. The near-perfect overlap confirms the accuracy of the quantum approach in capturing the singularities of the non-Hermitian admittance matrix.</sub>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a quantum-classical hybrid solver to overcome the computational bottleneck of analyzing resonance instabilities in massive power grids. By adapting the RVVQE algorithm for non-Hermitian matrices, it maps the grid's admittance matrix onto a QPU. This scalable approach promises to diagnose continental-scale electrical network failures currently beyond classical computing limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly applies advanced quantum algorithms (RVVQE) to a complex engineering problem involving open quantum systems dynamics, bridging theoretical quantum computation with large-scale physical infrastructure analysis.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Evaluating critical resonance modes of large-scale interacting power grids is computationally intractable for classical methods due to the need to diagonalize massive, non-Hermitian admittance matrices.

**Main result.** The proposed quantum-classical hybrid methodology successfully extracts critical resonance modal impedances with near-perfect accuracy compared to classical solutions on test systems.

**Method.** A novel Quantum-Classical Hybrid Resonance Mode Analysis (Q-RMA) framework is used, employing the Real Variance-based Variational Quantum Eigensolver (RVVQE) algorithm to handle the non-Hermitian nature of the grid admittance matrix ($\mathbf{Y}_{B,f}$).

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a multi-terminal power transmission grid, represented mathematically by its frequency-dependent, non-Hermitian admittance matrix ($\mathbf{Y}_{B,f}$), which governs dynamic instabilities.

**Key observables.** Critical resonance modal impedances ($Z_{cm,f}$) and eigenvalues of the admittance matrix across a wide frequency spectrum (100 Hz to 3000 Hz).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the grid ($n$-bus system), which dictates the required number of qubits ($\lceil\log_2 nceil$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Standard quantum eigensolvers are insufficient because they only handle Hermitian matrices, necessitating the use of RVVQE for the non-Hermitian $\mathbf{Y}_{B,f}$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the hybrid framework mapping $\mathbf{Y}_{B,f}$ onto a QPU using RVVQE. Figure 2 shows the iterative workflow (Q-RMA) across frequency sweeps. Figure 4 compares quantum RVVQE results against exact classical solutions for $|Z_{cm}|$.

**Paper structure.** The paper outlines the problem (harmonic distortions in grids), proposes the Q-RMA methodology involving matrix decomposition ($\mathbf{Y}_{B,f} = \mathbf{H}_f + i\mathbf{K}_f$), details the RVVQE implementation for non-Hermitian operators, and validates the approach by comparing quantum results to classical benchmarks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The proliferation of power electronics in multi-terminal transmission grids has increasingly led to harmonic distortions and dynamic instabilities. While Resonance Mode Analysis (RMA) provides deep insights into these system resonances, evaluating the critical modes of large-scale grids presents a severe computational bottleneck. Classical iterative techniques must continuously diagonalize massively high-dimensional, non-Hermitian admittance matrices across a wide frequency spectrum, a process that rapidly exhausts classical memory and processing limits. To overcome this scaling barrier, we propose a novel quantum-classical hybrid methodology that natively maps the transmission grid's admittance matrix onto a Quantum Processing Unit (QPU). Because the grid's matrix is non-Hermitian, standard quantum eigensolvers are insufficient; thus, we employ the Real Variance-based Variational Quantum Eigensolver (RVVQE) algorithm to accurately extract the complex eigenvalues that represent the system's modes. Validated against a standard 5-bus transmission system, the quantum-derived critical-resonance modal impedances demonstrate near-perfect alignment with the exact classical frequency responses. Crucially, by encoding the grid's state logarithmically into quantum memory, this methodology bypasses classical RAM limitations. The successful implementation of the RVVQE framework not only bridges the mathematical topologies of dissipative electrical grids and open quantum systems but also provides a profoundly scalable architecture capable of diagnosing resonance instabilities in massive, continental-scale networks that currently exceed classical computational boundaries.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates the Anomalous Hall effect in the altermagnetic material CrSb. Using advanced DFT calculations, the authors map out the electronic structure and topological properties of the material under various spin alignments. The key finding is that the AHE conductivity does not follow a simple linear relationship with magnetization for altermagnets, suggesting a necessary revision of current theoretical models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the rigorous calculation of topological invariants (Berry curvature) and their relation to transport coefficients provides advanced theoretical tools applicable to understanding quantum response in strongly correlated systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the Anomalous Hall effect (AHE) in the altermagnetic material CrSb, specifically questioning the conventional assumption that AHE conductivity must scale linearly with magnetization.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate through calculations that the anomalous Hall conductivity in altermagnets like CrSb does not necessarily exhibit a simple linear dependence on magnetization, challenging existing theoretical frameworks for magnetic materials.

**Method.** The research employs first-principles Density Functional Theory (DFT) coupled with maximally localized Wannier functions to calculate electronic spectra, Berry curvature, and topological surface states.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the altermagnetic compound CrSb. The theoretical framework involves calculating the AHE using Kubo formalism and analyzing the dependence of the anomalous Hall conductivity on various spin configurations and external fields.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall conductivity (sigma_xy), electronic band structure, phonon dispersion relations, geometrical Berry curvature, and topological surface states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis considers multiple magnetic configurations (e.g., coplanar vs. staggered spin orientations) and the dependence on the net magnetic moment.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes that standard AHE models based on simple linear magnetization scaling are insufficient for describing complex magnetic orders like altermagnetism.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures for different spin orientations, phonon dispersion relations, and crucially, show the calculated anomalous Hall conductivity dependence on various sublattice spin alignments, confirming non-linear behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the theoretical background of AHE in novel magnetic phases (altermagnetism), detailing advanced computational methods using MLWF/DFT, presenting calculations for CrSb's electronic and topological properties, and concluding with a re-evaluation of the linear magnetization dependence theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent development in the field of altermagnetism, and increased demand for the search of applications of anomalous hall effect have ushered in a new era for novel quantum phases in materials. Quantum materials previously anticipated to be scientifically predictable have unfolded novel properties that brought them into the spotlight. These manifestations have led us to rethink our understanding of existing classification of magnetic materials and preexisting notions about anomalous hall effect in the light of topologically nontrivial phases of matter. One such recent de- velopment lies in the novel class of alter-magnetic materials with prospect for quantum computing. In this article, we delineate the spin and orbital resolved electronic spectrum, mode-decomposed phonon dispersion relations, geometrical berry curvature and topological surface states and their implications on anomalous Hall conductivity in the promising altermagnetic compound CrSb. We further utilize first principles calculations coupled with computationally efficient maximally localized wannier states of numerous magnetic configurations of the altermagnet to simulate the effect of external fields and elucidate the fact that the linear behaviour of anomalous hall conductivity with magnetization does not necessarily hold true for all magnetic classes, such as altermagnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper determines the optimal way to approximate the complex conjugate of an unknown quantum isometry channel using only multiple calls to the original channel. By employing advanced tools like Schur-Weyl duality and SDP, the authors derive precise formulas for the achievable fidelity and establish a query complexity scaling law. This advances the understanding of resource limitations in black-box quantum process simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work establishes fundamental resource limitations for simulating complex operations (like conjugation) in quantum information theory, providing concrete query complexity bounds that are crucial for designing efficient quantum protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining the optimal deterministic protocol to approximate the complex conjugate of an unknown quantum isometry channel, $\overline{V}$, using only limited calls to the original channel $V$. This addresses fundamental questions about simulating inverse or adjoint operations with black-box access.

**Main result.** The authors derive a closed-form expression for the optimal fidelity and establish that a parallel protocol achieves this optimum. The query complexity required is found to be $n = \Theta(d[(D-d)/\epsilon + 1])$.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced techniques including semidefinite programming (SDP), Schur–Weyl duality, and the construction of optimal quantum circuits utilizing the quantum Schur transform and dual Clebsch–Gordan transforms.

**Model / system.** The model involves unknown isometry channels $V: \mathbb{C}^d	o\mathbb{C}^D$, which are generalized to superchannels. The analysis is framed in terms of channel operations and their representation via Stinespring dilations.

**Key observables.** Optimal fidelity ($F$), query complexity ($n$), and circuit complexity $O(	ext{poly}(D, 1/\epsilon))$ for the approximation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensions of the input ($d$) and output ($D$) spaces, the desired infidelity ($\epsilon$), and the number of copies ($k$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work focuses on *approximate* implementation, as exact complex conjugation is impossible with finite queries. Optimality proofs hold even against adaptive or indefinite-causal strategies.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the task (approximating $\overline{V}$ from $V$ calls) and detail the quantum circuit constructions based on the Schur transform and Clebsch–Gordan transforms.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives optimal fidelity bounds using SDP, proves the optimality of a parallel protocol via algebraic methods involving expectation values, extends results to multi-copy channels, and finally applies the framework to general unknown quantum superchannels.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Access to the complex conjugate of an unknown quantum channel is a useful resource in quantum oracle problems, motivating the question of how such access can be simulated using only a limited number of calls to the original channel. We determine the optimal deterministic protocol for approximately implementing the complex conjugate isometry $\overline{V}$ from $n$ uses of an unknown isometry channel $V: \mathbb{C}^d\to\mathbb{C}^D$. We derive a closed-form expression for the optimal fidelity and prove that a parallel protocol is optimal even among general quantum superchannels, including adaptive and indefinite-causal-order strategies. The formula implies a query complexity $n=Θ(d[(D-d)/ε+1])$ for achieving infidelity $ε$. We also present a circuit construction based on the quantum Schur transform and the dual Clebsch--Gordan transform, with circuit complexity $O(\mathrm{poly}(D,1/ε))$. This task is extended to the multi-copy case $V^{\otimes n}\mapsto \overline{V}^{\otimes k}$. For fixed $d<D$ and $k$, we show that the optimal fidelity for the multi-copy case is $1-kd(D-d)/n+o(n^{-1})$, and that this value is asymptotically attained by a parallel estimation-based protocol. Finally, combining the isometry protocol with random Stinespring dilations yields a protocol for complex conjugation of unknown rank-$r$ quantum channels whose query complexity is optimal up to a constant factor if the Kraus rank $r$ is constant.

</details>

### [Qubit Loss Inference with Stabilizer Codes without Leakage Detection Units](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29603v1)

**Authors:** Shin Nishio, Takeaki Uno, Fumiyoshi Kobayashi, Takahiko Satoh, Dan E. Browne  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.29603v1>

<sub>Figure 1. Loss correction protocol flows. The standard protocol to deal with loss errors is converting the loss error into an erasure at a known location via leakage detection units. Instead of leakage detection units, we propose a protocol with loss inference, which no longer requires running an additional circuit, which reduces overhead and eliminates a source of noise. After applying one of them, one can apply reloading, syndrome extraction, decoding, and Pauli error correction, or Pauli frame update. Note that surface code is used as an example, but both protocols can be easily generalized to arbitrary stabilizer codes by replacing the decoder.</sub>

Blue vertices represent surviving data qubits and the dotted light blue vertices represent the lost qubits. Each vertex on the face is the auxiliary qubit for a check operator. (Left) Precondition: Aij = 2 ≡0 (mod 2) so that two checks si and sj commute. (Center) For the case of single loss, |Aij ∩L| ≡1 (mod 2), and so two checks anticommute. This loss is detectable. (Right) For an even number of losses on Aij, two checks commute, therefore not detectable from the statistics of two stabilizer checks.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper proposes an alternative to using dedicated Leakage Detection Units (LDUs) to handle qubit loss in fault-tolerant quantum computing. By analyzing the syndrome data obtained from standard stabilizer measurements, the authors derive a mathematical condition based on anticommutation relations that proves when and how qubit loss can be inferred. This method promises reduced space-time overhead while maintaining low logical error rates for near-term hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a purely theoretical framework for mitigating physical errors in quantum computation by transforming hardware overheads (LDUs) into algorithmic/measurement-based diagnostics derived from fundamental algebraic properties of stabilizer codes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of qubit loss during quantum computation, aiming to infer the location of lost qubits directly from syndrome measurements without relying on dedicated hardware like Leakage Detection Units (LDUs).

**Main result.** It derives a sufficient condition for loss detectability based on the anticommutation of punctured stabilizer checks and shows that inference-based protocols can reduce logical error rates compared to LDU baselines in near-term regimes with less overhead.

**Method.** The authors develop theoretical criteria by analyzing the commutation relations between syndrome generators after accounting for lost qubits, formulating the problem as a maximum-likelihood/minimum set cover problem, and simulating performance on the surface code.

**Model / system.** The work is situated in the context of fault-tolerant quantum computation using stabilizer codes. The analysis models qubit loss under a non-entangling gate assumption for lost qubits, evaluating protocols on the rotated surface code.

**Key observables.** Logical error rate, anticommutation relations between punctured stabilizer checks ($s'_i s'_j = -s'_j s'_i$), and auxiliary qubit overhead.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low-to-moderate loss rates relevant to near-term hardware; the structure of the underlying stabilizer code (e.g., surface code).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The gate model assumes non-entangling behavior for gates involving a lost qubit, and the analysis relies on establishing sufficient conditions rather than necessary ones.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate flow diagrams comparing LDU vs. inference protocols, and show how commutation/anticommutation relations change based on the number of lost qubits relative to stabilizer generators.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the hardware overhead of LDUs to deriving a mathematical condition for loss detectability via syndrome data (based on anticommutation), formalizing this into an inference problem, and finally validating the protocol through noise simulations on specific platforms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Qubit loss occurs when the physical carrier of a qubit leaves the computational system without directly revealing the event's location. Such errors are a major obstacle to fault-tolerant quantum computation on platforms including photonic, neutral-atom, and trapped-ion systems. Loss locations are commonly identified using additional hardware operations such as leakage-detection units (LDUs), which introduce space-time overhead and may themselves become a source of error. We investigate whether qubit loss on stabilizer codes can instead be inferred from syndrome data obtained through standard repeated stabilizer measurements. Under a non-entangling model for gates involving a lost qubit, we derive a sufficient condition for loss detectability in general stabilizer codes. The condition is based on the emergence of anticommutation between stabilizer checks after their support on the lost qubits is removed. By using that condition, we formulate the exact loss-inference problem using the observed set of non-deterministic checks together with its maximum-likelihood formulation. We then relax the problem to the minimum set cover problem with a greedy heuristic algorithm. We evaluate the resulting inference and loss-correction protocols on the rotated surface code via circuit-level noise simulations for trapped-ion and neutral-atom platforms. On both platforms, inference-based and adaptive protocols reduce the logical error rate relative to a noisy-LDU baseline in the low-to-moderate loss-rate regime relevant to near-term hardware, while requiring fewer space-time overheads.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on evidence of successive electronic topological transitions in UPd2Al3 as it moves from an antiferromagnetic to a paramagnetic state under high magnetic fields. By analyzing sharp anomalies in thermoelectric power and Hall measurements, the authors argue for a strong Fermi surface reconstruction at the metamagnetic transition. The combination of transport diagnostics and theoretical modeling highlights how field-induced changes govern the electronic properties of heavy fermion systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work exemplifies how high-field magnetotransport measurements are crucial for mapping out complex electronic phase diagrams in strongly correlated materials, providing signatures (like sign changes and quantum oscillations) that point toward fundamental topological changes in the band structure.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates successive electronic topological transitions (ETTs) occurring in the antiferromagnetic state of the heavy fermion compound UPd2Al3 when subjected to a high external magnetic field.

**Main result.** The anomalies observed, particularly the sign change in thermoelectric power and Hall coefficient at the metamagnetic transition ($H_M$), strongly indicate a drastic Fermi surface reconstruction driven by band unfolding. This suggests that topological changes are key features of correlated electron behavior in this material.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements (thermoelectric power, Hall effect, dHvA oscillations) with theoretical analysis using renormalization perturbation theory to model the electronic structure changes.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the heavy fermion compound UPd2Al3, which exhibits antiferromagnetism and superconductivity. The focus is on understanding how its Fermi surface topology evolves across magnetic phase transitions (AF $\leftrightarrow$ PPM) under external fields.

**Key observables.** Thermoelectric power (Seebeck coefficient $S$), Hall coefficient ($ho_{xy}$), quantum oscillation frequencies, and effective masses ($m^*$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field strength up to 35 T; critical temperatures like Néel temperature ($T_N = 14.5 	ext{ K}$) and superconducting transition temperature ($T_{SC} = 2 	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The anomalies are attributed to complex topological changes in the Fermi surface driven by the Zeeman effect, suggesting a connection between magnetic field tuning and Lifshitz transitions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate temperature dependence of $S(T)$ showing kinks at $T_N$ and steps at $T_{SC}$; magnetoresistance/Hall measurements show anomalies at $H_M$; and FFT analysis reveals quantum oscillation frequencies above $H_M$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents transport measurements across the AF to PPM transition, using multiple diagnostics (TEP, Hall effect) to pinpoint the metamagnetic field ($H_M$). Theoretical modeling is then used to interpret these sharp changes as evidence of Fermi surface reconstruction.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report successive anomalies at low temperature in the magnetic field dependence of the thermoelectric signal in the heavy fermion compound UPd$_{2}$Al$_{3}$ inside the antiferromagnetic state up to the metamagnetic transition at $H_\text{M} =18$~T. Based on renormalisation perturbation theory and the partitioning of the $f$ orbitals into localized and delocalized parts, our analysis attributes these anomalies to complex topological changes of the Fermi surface driven by Zeeman effect. The observation of a sudden change of sign both in the thermoelectric power and in the Hall coefficient at $H_\text{M}$ in addition to the appearance of large quantum oscillations in the thermoelectric power above $H_\text{M}$ indicate a strong Fermi surface reconstruction at the metamagnetic transition due to the unfolding of the electronic bands.

</details>

<a id="paper-2607.28845"></a>
### [Transcorrelated Random-Phase Approximation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28845v1)

**Authors:** Abdallah Ammar, Enzo Monino, Anthony Scemama, Emmanuel Giner, Pierre-François Loos  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.28845v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the Transcorrelated Random-Phase Approximation (TC-RPA) to enhance electronic structure calculations by incorporating explicit three-body correlation effects via a Jastrow factor. It demonstrates that TC-RPA provides substantial accuracy improvements for ground-state energies in atoms and molecules, making it an efficient tool for ground-state energetics. However, the authors caution that state-specific optimization is needed to accurately model excited states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles highly correlated electronic structure problems using advanced many-body techniques (RPA extensions), which is central to understanding chemical bonding and material properties relevant to AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is improving electronic structure calculations by addressing the slow basis set convergence of standard Random-Phase Approximation (RPA) due to short-range electron correlation effects.

**Main result.** The Transcorrelated RPA (TC-RPA) significantly improves accuracy and accelerates basis set convergence for ground-state correlation energies, but only shows marginal improvement for vertical excitation energies.

**Method.** The authors extend the RPA formalism to a non-Hermitian Transcorrelated (TC) Hamiltonian by incorporating three-body interactions via a Jastrow correlation factor. They apply this using both direct and exchange variants of the approximation.

**Model / system.** Calculations are performed for atoms (He, Ne) and small molecules (H2O, NH3, CH4, H2CO). The theory utilizes an Equation-of-Motion approach applied to the non-Hermitian TC Hamiltonian, requiring a biorthogonal formalism.

**Key observables.** Ground-state correlation energies and vertical excitation energies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Jastrow correlation factor, which is optimized for the ground state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation noted is that optimizing the Jastrow factor only for the ground state limits its effectiveness in describing excited states.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; however, a comparison figure mentioned suggests TC-RPA accelerates basis set convergence compared to standard RPA calculations for ground-state energies.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the formalism by extending RPA to the non-Hermitian TC Hamiltonian. It then applies this method to calculate correlation and excitation energies for various molecules, culminating in a comparison of results showing differential improvements between ground and excited states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We extend the random-phase approximation (RPA) to the non-Hermitian transcorrelated (TC) Hamiltonian, which explicitly includes three-body interactions generated by a Jastrow correlation factor. We consider both the direct RPA (dRPA) and RPA with exchange (RPAx). We apply the resulting TC-dRPA and TC-RPAx methods to calculate ground-state correlation energies and vertical excitation energies for atoms (\ce{He} and \ce{Ne}) and small molecules (\ce{H2O}, \ce{NH3}, \ce{CH4}, and \ce{H2CO}). For ground-state correlation energies, the TC treatment substantially improves accuracy and accelerates basis set convergence, reducing errors by nearly an order of magnitude relative to conventional RPA calculations. By contrast, it yields only marginal improvements in vertical excitation energies. We attribute this limited effect to the ground-state optimization of the Jastrow factor, which does not adequately capture the distinct electronic character of excited states. These results establish TC-RPA as an accurate and computationally efficient approach to ground-state energetics, while highlighting the need for state-specific Jastrow optimization to achieve reliable descriptions of excited states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces FedQML-Edge, a federated learning framework that uses compact quantum feature sketches to process raw traffic data from roadside units (RSUs). By transmitting only small classifier updates derived from these quantum sketches, the method drastically reduces communication bandwidth requirements while maintaining high accuracy for critical tasks like predicting stable traffic flow.

**Why it may be interesting.** The integration of quantum sketching into a practical, communication-constrained edge AI task like traffic management is novel. It demonstrates how quantum principles can solve real-world data transmission bottlenecks in physical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a compact, privacy-preserving learning method for roadside units (RSUs) to determine traffic stability in connected autonomous vehicle corridors under bandwidth constraints.

**Main result.** The proposed method significantly reduces communication overhead while maintaining high predictive accuracy, achieving state-of-the-art performance compared to large classical MLPs on both NGSIM and SUMO benchmarks.

**Method.** It employs a federated quantum feature-sketching pipeline where raw traffic data is mapped into a compact Pauli expectation value sketch using a fixed quantum circuit. This sketch then feeds a locally trained logistic classifier, with only the small classifier update being shared during federated averaging.

**Model / system.** The system involves RSUs observing six key traffic features (e.g., vehicle count, mean speed) and sending inputs to a remote quantum service for sketch generation. The learning process is decentralized (Federated Learning), using the sketch as a low-dimensional feature representation for classification.

**Key observables.** Test log loss, Gate Recall, Update Payload Size (bytes), Pauli expectation values ($\mathbf{x}_{e,t}$)

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the classifier update (144 bytes) is constant and independent of traffic volume; the sketch dimension is 17.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that a fixed quantum feature map can provide sufficient non-linearity for classification while keeping communication updates minimal. Raw observations remain private at the RSU.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the computation and communication workflow (RSU $ightarrow$ Quantum Service $ightarrow$ RSU $ightarrow$ Aggregator) and show loss curves comparing FedQML to classical baselines over training rounds on NGSIM/SUMO.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the problem setup in CAVs, introduces the FedQML-Edge methodology (sketching via Pauli expectations), presents performance evaluations using both NGSIM and SUMO datasets, compares results against various classical feature sets and MLPs, and discusses hardware feasibility on IBM Fez.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Roadside units (RSUs) supporting connected and autonomous vehicle corridors need compact models to decide when cooperative maneuvers should be rewarded, deferred, or disabled. Raw sensor streams and neural network weight checkpoints are poorly suited to bandwidth-limited, privacy-sensitive roadside learning. This paper presents $\texttt{FedQML-Edge}$, a federated quantum feature-sketching pipeline for traffic-stability gating. Each RSU constructs a traffic-state summary and sends circuit inputs to a quantum computer; Pauli expectations form a nonlinear sketch processed by a logistic classifier. Only classifier updates are shared with an aggregator, whose head supports reward gating. Raw observations, vehicle records, event traces, and quantum sketches remain private. We evaluate the method using NGSIM trajectories, SUMO predictive gating with sensing noise, and IBM Quantum hardware. On NGSIM, the Pauli sketch reduces test log loss by $14.4\%$ relative to the strongest matched classical sketch. On SUMO, it approaches larger MLPs in stable-window recall while using $7-28$ times less communication per round.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops a rigorous framework to classify interacting Symmetry Protected Topological (SPT) phases in crystalline systems. It proposes an equivariant Free to Interacting (FTI) map that corrects previous classification schemes by prioritizing full crystallographic equivariance over the established Crystalline Equivalence Principle. This advances our understanding of topological order in materials with crystal symmetries.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep mathematical machinery (equivariant K-theory) to classify topological phases in crystalline materials, which is crucial for understanding symmetry protection beyond simple lattice models relevant to AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses developing a natural classification scheme for interacting Symmetry Protected Topological (SPT) phases, specifically resolving the conflict between established crystalline equivalence principles and results derived from free fermions.

**Main result.** The authors propose a fully equivariant Free to Interacting (FTI) map that respects full crystallographic equivariance, arguing this structure is more natural than previous constructions based on the potentially flawed Crystalline Equivalence Principle (CEP).

**Method.** The work employs advanced algebraic topology tools, including equivariant K-theory and Anderson duality, to construct and analyze a candidate FTI map connecting free fermion theories to interacting SPT phases.

**Model / system.** The focus is on crystalline SPT phases characterized by an internal symmetry group H and a crystallographic symmetry group G. The analysis compares classifications derived from invertible field theories against those arising from weak free fermions.

**Key observables.** Classification invariants such as $\pi_0(	ext{Sd}(H))$ for interacting phases, and $KO^{d+s-2}_P(T^d)$ for free fermions; the kernel and cokernel of the proposed FTI map are key diagnostics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality ($d$), internal symmetry structure ($H$), and crystallographic point group action ($P$) are central parameters governing the classification.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that full equivariance with respect to the spatial symmetry group $P$ should take precedence over maintaining the Crystalline Equivalence Principle (CEP).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first identifying the failure of the CEP for free fermions, then generalizing the Freed and Hopkins ansatz to a fully equivariant version, and finally constructing and analyzing the resulting natural equivariant FTI map.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Freed and Hopkins developed an ansatz for classifying interacting SPT phases using invertible field theories with a natural Free to Interacting (FTI) map from free fermion phases. This ansatz has been generalized to include crystalline phases and a crystalline equivalence principle (CEP). However, motivated by failure of the CEP for weak free fermions and the FTI, here we generalize the original Freed and Hopkins ansatz to a fully equivariant version for symmorphic crystallographic symmetries and show there is a natural equivariant FTI map from symmorphic crystalline weak free fermions. We further discuss why this equivariant ansatz is both mathematically and physically more natural than the spatial symmetry extension by Freed and Hopkins and why full equivariance should hold over the CEP.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces InferQ, a comprehensive benchmark designed to test quantum circuit simulation by translating computations into SQL workloads executable on RDBMSs. It systematically generates diverse circuits and extracts rich features, demonstrating that database engines can offer competitive performance metrics like memory usage compared to standard simulators. This work opens the door for data-centric optimization of quantum algorithms by predicting the best execution backend.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on database performance, the systematic benchmarking of quantum circuits provides a novel computational resource analysis framework that could inform hardware-aware algorithm design or compiler optimization for future quantum computing architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors developed a systematic benchmark, InferQ, to evaluate quantum circuit simulation performance when executed using Relational Database Management Systems (RDBMSs) compared to standard simulators.

**Main result.** InferQ demonstrated that RDBMSs can achieve better peak memory usage than Qiskit Aer on over 50% of tested circuits, and machine learning models trained on its features can accurately predict the optimal simulation engine.

**Method.** The method involves generating general, compositional quantum circuits, translating each simulation task into an RDBMS-ready SQL workload (tensor contractions), and extracting comprehensive static, graph, and SQL features for analysis.

**Model / system.** The system under study is the quantum circuit model, where states are represented by complex vectors. The computation is simulated either via standard quantum simulators or by compiling the operations into join-and-aggregate tensor contractions executed within an RDBMS.

**Key observables.** Peak memory usage, execution runtime, classification accuracy (for predicting optimal backend), and various circuit/SQL features (e.g., graph diameter, number of JOINs).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The benchmark covers a large dataset of 202,975 circuits generated by composing subcircuits from templates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that quantum circuit simulation can be systematically mapped onto SQL workloads suitable for database analysis. The ML predictions rely on the completeness and diversity of the extracted features.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate feature distributions (static, graph, SQL), performance comparisons between RDBMSs and Qiskit Aer, and machine learning model performance metrics showing high prediction accuracy.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces InferQ to address the lack of systematic benchmarks. It details circuit generation via template composition, workload extraction into SQL, feature engineering (static/graph/SQL), comparative experiments across multiple RDBMSs vs. Qiskit Aer, and finally, using ML models to predict optimal execution backends.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent work suggests that relational database management systems (RDBMSs) can execute quantum circuit simulation by compiling the simulation into SQL workloads (primarily join-and-aggregate tensor contractions). While early results are promising, they largely focus on a narrow set of highly structured circuits and offer limited support for systematic database research, such as query optimization, physical design, and engine-level evaluation across a broad range of circuits. We present InferQ, a database-oriented benchmark for quantum circuit simulation. InferQ generates general, compositional circuits by assembling subcircuits from a set of circuit templates, emits each simulation task as an RDBMS-ready SQL workload, and extracts circuit and query features (static, graph, SQL, and dynamic) for workload characterization. InferQ also releases a large dataset of 202,975 circuits online, with a web-based viewer to support searching, filtering, and downloading circuits and feature records. In experiments across RDBMS engines (PostgreSQL, SQLite, DuckDB, and Umbra) and the widely used Qiskit Aer simulator, we find that RDBMSs achieve better peak memory usage than Qiskit Aer on more than 50% of the circuits generated by InferQ. Moreover, using InferQ features, lightweight machine learning models (linear and tree-based models) can accurately predict when SQL execution is preferable (with accuracy up to 95.3% for runtime and 97.4% for memory), enabling data-centric simulator selection and opening the door to principled optimization of SQL-based quantum circuit simulation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel bounded-error quantum algorithm to compute modular factorials, $n! \pmod{p}$ and $\pmod{p^2}$, with an expected time complexity that surpasses classical limits under certain conditions. The breakthrough relies on developing a quantum method to exactly reconstruct complex algebraic objects, specifically Jacobi sums, in a compact form suitable for computation modulo prime ideals.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about physical Hamiltonians, the development of quantum algorithms for fundamental number-theoretic problems like modular factorials demonstrates the potential reach of quantum computation beyond chemistry or materials science. The techniques used to handle exponentially large algebraic numbers are highly relevant to theoretical quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing an efficient quantum algorithm to compute modular factorials, specifically $n! \pmod{p}$ and $n! \pmod{p^2}$, for a prime $p$. A secondary technical challenge involves reconstructing Jacobi sums modulo prime ideals.

**Main result.** The authors present a bounded-error quantum algorithm achieving an expected time complexity of $\widetilde{O}(q\sqrt{c} + p/q)$ for computing $n! \pmod{p}$, which breaks the classical square-root barrier under specific divisor conditions. This technique is extended to compute factorials modulo $p^2$.

**Method.** The core method involves using quantum algorithms to reconstruct Jacobi sums in a compact algebraic form, circumventing the need for explicit construction due to their potentially enormous size. The overall computation combines this specialized number-theoretic subroutine with techniques derived from Young's congruences and multinomial coefficient identities.

**Model / system.** The paper is purely theoretical, dealing with computational complexity bounds applied to number theory problems involving modular arithmetic over finite fields $\mathbb{F}_p$ and rings $\mathbb{Z}/p^2\mathbb{Z}$. The 'system' is the mathematical structure of prime moduli and associated character sums.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the values $n! \pmod{p}$ and $n! \pmod{p^2}$, alongside intermediate quantities like Jacobi sums $J_a(\chi)$ and ratios of multinomial coefficients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the prime modulus $p$, a divisor $q$ of $p-1$, and the integer $n$. The complexity depends critically on balancing terms involving $q$ and $p/q$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The algorithm relies on the existence of a suitable divisor $q | (p-1)$ to achieve its stated time bounds. Furthermore, several quantum routines used (e.g., for ideal generation) are assumed to function correctly.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general problem of modular factorials to the technical core: reconstructing Jacobi sums using a three-step quantum process involving PIP and S-unit algorithms. It then applies this machinery, leveraging number theory congruences (like those relating multinomial coefficients to Jacobi sums), to derive the final complexity bounds for $n! \pmod{p}$ and $n! \pmod{p^2}$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give a bounded-error quantum algorithm that, given a prime $p$, a divisor $q\mid(p-1)$, and an integer $0<n<p$, computes $n!\bmod p$ in expected time $\widetilde{O}(q^c+\sqrt{p/q})$ for some absolute constant $c\ge 1$. When $p-1$ has a divisor of size $q\approx p^{1/(2c+1)}$, this gives the exponent $c/(2c+1)<1/2$. To our knowledge, this is the first algorithm to break the exponent $1/2$ barrier for modular factorials under such a divisor promise. The main technical ingredient is a quantum algorithm that reconstructs the relevant Jacobi sum exactly in compact algebraic form, with polynomial dependence on $q$ and $\log p$. We further extend the same asymptotic bound to the computation of $n!\bmod p^2$, uniformly over $0\le n<p^2$. At $n=p-1$, this determines the Wilson quotient $\frac{(p-1)!+1}{p}\pmod p$. We conjecture that the condition $q\mid(p-1)$ is a technical limitation of the present method rather than an inherent obstruction, and that a uniform quantum algorithm exists for all primes.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes optimal sample complexity bounds for solving planted inference problems within noisy kXOR models. By introducing a normalized version of the Kikuchi hierarchy, the authors prove that previously conjectured sharp trade-offs are achievable without logarithmic losses. The work also provides a quantum algorithm demonstrating a significant speedup over classical spectral methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly mathematical and rooted in coding theory/ML, the rigorous establishment of sharp information-theoretic bounds (like those governing signal detection) is foundational to understanding complexity limits in physical systems modeled by random constraints.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses establishing sharp theoretical bounds for planted inference problems, specifically detecting or recovering a hidden low-rank signal within noisy $k$XOR constraints.

**Main result.** They prove that a normalized variant of the Kikuchi hierarchy achieves the conjectured optimal trade-off between signal strength and sample size with no logarithmic loss for detection, weak recovery, and strong refutation.

**Method.** The core methods involve spectral analysis using the normalized sparse Kikuchi matrix, sharp counting of closed walks in its trace expansion, and establishing matching lower bounds via low-degree polynomial techniques.

**Model / system.** The model is planted noisy $k$XOR, where constraints are defined by clauses specifying the parity of $k$ out of $n$ Boolean variables. The analysis extends to general fixed predicates and local planting laws.

**Key observables.** Signal strength ($ho$), Hierarchy Level ($\ell$), Number of Clauses ($m$), Detection/Recovery accuracy, Refutation certificate degree.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Arity $k \ge 3$, Bias $ho$, Level $\ell$, Sample size $m$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The main improvement over prior work is the normalization technique used to overcome polylogarithmic losses in spectral analyses, allowing matching of the conjectured sharp trade-off curve.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon establishing the theoretical framework using the Kikuchi hierarchy and then presents results for detection, weak recovery, and strong refutation, culminating in a quantum algorithm achieving a quartic speedup.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Planted noisy $k$XOR and the strong refutation of random $k$XOR are governed by a conjectured trade-off between signal strength and time: Level $\ell$ of the Kikuchi hierarchy should achieve the smooth curve \begin{equation*}   m\ \gtrsim\ ρ^{-2}n^{k/2}/\ell^{k/2-1}\ \text{clauses}   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad   \text{solvable in time }n^{O(\ell)}, \end{equation*} where $ρ$ is the bias of the planted signal or, for refutation, the target advantage. However, every spectral analysis of sparse $k$XOR to date loses polylogarithmic factors against this curve, a loss that enters the exponent of the running time.   We show that a normalized variant of the Kikuchi hierarchy achieves the sharp conjectured trade-off, with no logarithmic loss, at every arity $k\ge3$. At the scale above, our algorithms achieve strong detection, weak recovery, and strong refutation; an additional cleanup step boosts weak recovery to exact recovery, and the refutation certificates yield sum-of-squares proofs of degree $O_k(\ell)$. We also prove matching lower bounds in the same model. The inference and refutation upper bounds transfer to more general planting laws and predicates. Finally, we give a quantum algorithm that achieves a quartic speedup over the classical spectral algorithms for detection and weak recovery.   The proofs rest on two key ingredients: a normalization of the sparse Kikuchi matrix, and a sharp count of the closed walks in its trace expansion. We use a closely related trace-walk count to prove Feige's 2008 hypergraph Moore bound conjecture in a companion paper.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how logical errors accumulate when performing lattice surgery across physically separated quantum modules. It models the merge operation using a detailed noise framework incorporating imperfections in shared entangled links. The results offer crucial, quantitative guidelines for designing fault-tolerant distributed quantum computers.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides concrete quantitative metrics (thresholds and fidelity requirements) that bridge theoretical quantum error correction concepts with realistic hardware constraints imposed by networking multiple physical processors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is characterizing logical errors that accumulate during distributed quantum computation, specifically focusing on the merge operation in lattice surgery.

**Main result.** The work derives practical insights by providing quantitative estimates for required local-gate fidelities and tolerable entangled-pair fidelities necessary for fault-tolerant operations in a distributed architecture.

**Method.** The analysis employs Minimum-Weight Perfect Matching (MWPM) decoding on an H-shaped spacetime diagram to estimate logical error rates under a phenomenological noise model.

**Model / system.** The system is based on distributed quantum computing using smaller modules networked by shared entangled pairs, implementing the planar surface code via lattice surgery techniques.

**Key observables.** Logical Z errors in the XX merge operation; logical error rates ($\mathfrak{q}_{	ext{seam}}$); and operational thresholds.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Surface-code distance ($d$); Bell-pair noise scaling factor ($k$); local CNOT gate fidelities ($\epsilon_{cx}, \epsilon_m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a phenomenological noise model that explicitly accounts for contributions from imperfect Bell pairs, local gates, idling, and readout errors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the rotated surface code structure, entanglement-assisted nonlocal CNOT gates, the lattice surgery circuit for logical CNOTs, and the H-shaped spacetime diagram detailing the merge operation history.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the distributed setting to analyzing the core merge operation using syndrome measurements, developing a comprehensive noise model incorporating non-local errors, and finally deriving scaling laws for the resulting logical error rate.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Distributed quantum computing offers a scalable alternative to monolithic quantum processors by networking smaller quantum modules through shared entangled pairs. A central challenge in this setting is that inter-module quantum operations are typically noisier than intra-module local gates, which introduces additional noise into the system. In this work, we analyze distributed lattice surgery under heterogeneous noise conditions, focusing in particular on the merge operation as one of its fundamental subroutines. Specifically, we discuss the XX merge operation between two rotated surface-code patches hosted on two different quantum processors. We characterize logical errors in the resulting H-shaped spacetime diagram and estimate thresholds using a minimum-weight perfect matching (MWPM) decoder. We use a phenomenological noise model and derive distinct bulk and seam error rates to approximate a circuit-level noise model that includes contributions from local CNOT gates, noisy entangled pairs, idle errors, and readout errors. Our results provide practical insights into selecting the optimal surface-code distance, establishing target local-gate fidelities, and determining the tolerable entangled-pair fidelity required for logical operations in a distributed architecture.

</details>
