**Summary.** This paper introduces an advanced autotuning framework that uses supervised machine learning to automatically select and order the best sequence of transpiler optimization passes for quantum circuits. By training a predictive model on extensive benchmark data, it significantly outperforms default compiler settings in reducing costly two-qubit gates. This advances the practical usability of quantum hardware by making compilation more efficient.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical compilation bottleneck in quantum computing, which is crucial for any theoretical physicist simulating or designing algorithms for near-term hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is determining the optimal combination of transpiler passes for quantum circuit optimization, as fixed default settings often fail to achieve maximum performance.

**Main result.** The proposed ML-selected pass combinations consistently match or exceed existing Qiskit optimizations, achieving an average additional reduction of 19.1% - 32.4% in two-qubit gates.

**Method.** The authors developed an autotuning pipeline using supervised machine learning (XGBoost) trained on a dataset generated via feature-model based sampling across thousands of benchmark circuits.

**Model / system.** The system under study is quantum circuits implemented via gate-based programming models, optimized for execution on Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) devices using the Qiskit framework.

**Key observables.** Maximum reduction in two-qubit gates; circuit properties like gate count and depth are used as explanatory variables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimization goal is minimizing errors associated with two-qubit gates on NISQ hardware.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that a predictive ML model can accurately generalize the performance gains of specific transpiler pass combinations to unseen quantum circuits.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the stages of the Qiskit transpiler pipeline. Figure 2 details the six-step autotuning methodology, from search space formalization to SHAP analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper outlines a systematic approach: first, defining the problem (combinatorial explosion); second, generating data via feature-model sampling; third, training an ML predictor in a learning-to-rank setting; and finally, validating the model's predictions against empirical benchmarks using SHAP for interpretability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum software engineering is an emerging research field focusing on efficiently embedding the quantum programming paradigm into existing software ecosystems. A key aspect of this field is the realization of quantum algorithms using gate-based programming and the subsequent low-level optimization of the resulting quantum circuits, a process that is commonly performed by so-called transpilation pipelines. One significant challenge in these pipelines is determining which optimizations to apply to a given circuit. This decision is usually based on fixed default configurations that are uniformly applied to all circuits, frequently resulting in missed opportunities for more aggressive circuit optimization. In this work, we tackle this challenge by applying autotuning with supervised machine learning to develop an automated method for selection of transpiler passes. To train our machine-learning models, we employ feature-model based sampling to generate a representative dataset that examines how different combinations of Qiskit transpiler passes perform across thousands of circuits drawn from the state-of-the-art benchmarking suite MQT Bench. Using these data, we build a predictive model extension for the Qiskit transpilation pipeline that uses a machine learning model to automatically select combinations of transpiler passes aiming to achieve a maximum reduction in two-qubit gates. Our empirical evaluation shows that the combinations selected by our model are never outperformed by Qiskit's optimization levels, achieve on average an additional 19.1$\%$ - 32.4$\%$ reduction in two-qubit gates, and for some circuits finds reductions of up to $95.8\%$ in cases where Qiskit achieves no reduction at all.

</details>

**Summary.** Researchers demonstrate the formation of propagating exciton-polaritons by integrating TMD monolayers into photonic waveguides. They use low-temperature spectroscopy to observe characteristic spectral splittings in the A exciton, confirming strong coupling between excitons and the waveguide mode. The findings are supported by detailed theoretical modeling using transfer matrix methods, advancing on-chip quantum light sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics in solid-state photonics, providing experimental evidence and theoretical frameworks for engineered light-matter coupling (exciton-photon interaction) crucial for integrated quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates the emergence and properties of propagating exciton-polaritons formed by coupling excitons within Transition Metal Dichalcogenides (TMDs) to optical waveguide modes.

**Main result.** Strong coupling leads to observable spectral shifts, redshifts, or splittings in the A exciton resonance, confirming the formation of hybrid exciton-polaritons whose properties can be modeled theoretically.

**Method.** The study employs a combination of low-temperature optical spectroscopy ($\mu$PL) measurements across various geometries and theoretical modeling using the transfer matrix method for waveguide dispersion relations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a hybrid structure comprising TMD monolayers (e.g., MoSe2, WSe2) encapsulated by hBN, placed atop femtosecond-laser-written surface waveguides embedded in fused silica chips.

**Key observables.** Spectral shifts/splittings of the A exciton ($\Delta E$), polariton energy levels (upper and lower branches), group velocity ($v_g$), and propagation distance ($l$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** hBN encapsulation thickness, temperature (down to 4 K), waveguide width, and material-specific exciton/trion energies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that observed peak splitting is due to strong coupling; theoretical models are highly simplified approximations used to highlight physical trends rather than predict exact values.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the hybrid structure schematic, $\mu$PL spectra showing polarization and temperature dependence of exciton/polariton features, and theoretical dispersion relations derived from transfer matrix calculations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the experimental platform and observing spectral anomalies (splitting/redshift) in TMDs. This observation is then theoretically modeled using waveguide theory to confirm the polaritonic nature, followed by detailed analysis of temperature and geometry dependence.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Integrating few-layer materials into photonic circuits is a promising concept for novel on-chip photonic applications. We incorporate transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) with femtosecond-laser-written surface waveguides, which are embedded in fused silica chips. Our novel low-temperature optical spectroscopy setup enables coupling to the waveguide and simultaneous focus from the top to the TMD layer for a distinct excitation and collection of micro-photoluminescence ($μ$PL) signals in several measurement geometries. Along these lines, we observe spectral changes of the A exciton for encapsulated TMD monolayers when capturing the $μ$PL signal propagating through the waveguide. Depending on the thickness of the encapsulation with hexagonal boron nitride (hBN), these changes manifest as energetic redshifts of the A exciton, or even a splitting of the A exciton into two components. We attribute this behavior to strong coupling of the waveguide mode and the exciton in the sample, giving rise to the formation of propagating exciton-polaritons. Our interpretation is supported by calculations for a simplified model of a slab waveguide in the vicinity of an exciton by using the transfer matrix method. Having proven to be a highly adaptable framework for the study of propagating polaritons, our experimental platform likewise holds great promise for harnessing the unique properties of exciton-polaristons in integrated photonic circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes electronic tunneling through a quantum well strongly coupled to an optical cavity mode. Using non-equilibrium Green's functions, the authors predict that polariton interactions manifest as distinct inelastic sidebands in the current-voltage characteristics. This offers a powerful spectroscopic tool for characterizing light-matter coupling in semiconductor heterostructures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges concepts from cavity QED (polaritons, strong coupling) with open quantum system transport theory (NEGF), providing a concrete theoretical framework for observing light-matter interaction effects in solid-state electronics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates electronic transport through a quantum well strongly coupled to a cavity photon mode, aiming to detect observable signatures of polariton effects in charge current.

**Main result.** The current-voltage characteristics exhibit inelastic sidebands associated with resonant and anti-resonant polariton emission, providing a realistic detection route for cavity-induced modifications of charge transport.

**Method.** The analysis employs the non-equilibrium Green's function (NEGF) formalism within the Meir–Wingreen framework to derive compact expressions for the current.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a doped quantum well coupled to the fundamental TM mode of a double-metal cavity. The interaction is described by a Hamiltonian including electronic, cavity, and light-matter coupling terms.

**Key observables.** Current–voltage characteristics ($J$-$V$), inelastic sidebands associated with polariton emission ($\omega_\pm$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strong coupling regime (coupling strength > loss rates), slow-injection regime (carrier injection rate < electronic broadening).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations rely on the NEGF formalism, utilizing approximations like the polariton-pole approximation and pseudo-equilibrium assumptions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $J$-$V$ characteristics showing additional inelastic peaks when coupling is non-zero, comparing coupled vs. uncoupled transport.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the Hamiltonian and using NEGF to derive current expressions; it then applies approximations like the polariton-pole model to calculate radiative self-energies ($\Sigma_{	ext{rad},n}$) and finally derives separate elastic ($J_{	ext{el}}$) and inelastic ($J_{	ext{in}}$) current components.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate electronic transport through a doped quantum well strongly interacting with a photonic mode confined in a double-metal cavity. Using a nonequilibrium Green's function formalism, we derive compact expressions for the current valid for arbitrary collective light--matter coupling strengths exceeding the relevant loss rates. We show that cavity polaritons leave observable transport signatures when the carrier injection rate is smaller than the cavity-induced electronic broadening. In this regime, the current--voltage characteristics exhibit inelastic sidebands associated with resonant and anti-resonant polariton emission, which are strongly enhanced under resonant illumination. Our results provide a realistic route to detecting cavity-induced modifications of charge transport in semiconductor heterostructures.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate a controlled measurement of the transition between charge-dominated (Coulomb Blockade) and phase-dominated (Josephson) transport regimes in a Josephson junction using an STM tip. By continuously tuning $E_J/E_C$, they confirm the smooth crossover, validating advanced theoretical models like qIZ against direct experimental observation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a direct experimental realization of controlling quantum fluctuations in superconducting circuits, which is highly relevant for understanding dissipation mechanisms in superconducting qubits and quantum computing architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to monitor and understand the smooth quantum-to-classical transition of Cooper pair flow in a Josephson junction by continuously tuning the ratio of Josephson energy ($E_J$) to charging energy ($E_C$).

**Main result.** The experiment successfully traced the full crossover from the Dynamical Coulomb Blockade (DCB) regime to the coherent, dissipationless tunneling (DCT) regime in a single device.

**Method.** The research combines STM-based experiments to tune junction parameters with advanced theoretical modeling using Multiple Andreev Reflections (MARs) and the quantum Ivanchenko-Zil’berman (qIZ) model.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a superconducting tunnel junction whose behavior is governed by the competition between $E_J$ and $E_C$. The analysis uses theoretical frameworks like MAR theory and the qIZ model to describe current flow across different regimes.

**Key observables.** Current-voltage ($I(V)$) characteristics, switching current ($I_S$) vs. conductance ($G_N$), and phase rotation per tunneling Cooper pair ($\Delta\phi/\Delta n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $E_J/E_C$, the Stewart-McCumber parameter ($eta_C < 1$ for overdamped regime), and junction conductance ($G_N$) are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Theoretical models assume an ohmic environment impedance, a sinusoidal current-phase relation (which breaks down at high $G_N$), and the applicability of specific approximations like Markovianity in certain regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the crossover of switching current from quadratic to linear dependence on conductance ($I_S$ vs. $G_N$) and illustrate MAR fits used to extract channel configurations and track phase rotation evolution.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the experimental tunability of $E_J/E_C$, then analyzing the resulting current-phase relation using MARs, and finally comparing theoretical predictions (qIZ vs. P(E)-theory) against the observed smooth crossover between DCB and DCT regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum mechanics constrains conjugate observables from being simultaneously measurable with arbitrary precision. In a Josephson junction, these are the transferred electric charge and the quantum-mechanical phase difference between the superconducting domains. Which of them fluctuates determines the supercurrent: a dissipative trickle of single Cooper pairs in one limit, a coherent dissipationless flow in the other. Bridging both regimes in one device remained elusive because the Josephson and charging energies are fixed at fabrication. Here, we use the tunable tunnel junction of a scanning tunneling microscope at millikelvin temperature to vary their ratio continuously over many orders of magnitude. In this way, we monitor the smooth transition between incoherent and coherent Cooper pair flow in a single junction, revealing the quantum-to-classical transition in a controlled way.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers developed an optomechanical technique to actively tune the nonlinearity of graphene resonators by using photothermal heating within an optical cavity. By controlling the incident laser power, they demonstrated continuous control over the Duffing nonlinearity, enabling transitions between hardening and softening mechanical behaviors. This establishes a versatile platform for engineering nonlinear dynamics in 2D nanoelectromechanical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an excellent example of coupling mechanical resonators to optical fields for active control, which is central to optomechanics and open quantum systems research. The ability to engineer nonlinearities on demand opens pathways for novel quantum sensing or non-linear cavity QED setups.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to achieve active, reversible, in situ tuning of the nonlinear nanomechanical response of graphene resonators.

**Main result.** The study successfully demonstrated continuous control over both the strength and sign of the Duffing nonlinearity by manipulating the optical probe power and cavity depth.

**Method.** An optomechanical approach was used, exploiting photothermal heating to induce dynamic changes in the mechanical restoring force of graphene membranes.

**Model / system.** The physical system involves graphene membranes suspended over a reflective substrate forming an optical cavity. The dynamics are modeled using a driven Duffing oscillator ($\ddot{x} + 2\zeta \dot{x} + k_{eff}(P_\lambda, x_{eq}) x + \dots = F \cos(\omega dt)$).

**Key observables.** Resonance frequency ($\omega^2$), effective Duffing coefficient ($eta_{	ext{eff}}$), and the equilibrium position ($x_{eq}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Optical probe power ($P_\lambda$), cavity depth, and laser wavelengths (e.g., 632.8 nm, 488 nm).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The photothermal response is treated as quasi-static; the analysis involves expanding tension terms around equilibrium.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the optical cavity setup, frequency tuning vs. power/position, and nonlinear stiffness tuning vs. power/position.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical platform (optical cavity) to modeling the resulting dynamics as a Duffing oscillator whose coefficients are controlled by external laser parameters, culminating in measurements demonstrating tunable nonlinearity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Graphene resonators exhibit unique mechanical and electrical properties, making them promising candidates for next-generation sensing applications. Here, we introduce an optomechanical approach that enables active, reversible, in situ tuning of the nonlinear nanomechanical response of graphene resonators. By exploiting photothermal heating and the resulting optomechanical interaction between the mechanical oscillations and the optical cavity formed by a graphene membrane suspended over a reflective substrate, we achieve dynamic control over both the linear restoring force and the nonlinear dynamic response of the resonator. We show that the strength and sign of the Duffing nonlinearity can be continuously tuned through the optical probe power and cavity depth, enabling transitions between hardening and softening behavior as well as near-complete suppression of geometric nonlinearities. Our results establish a versatile platform for on-demand engineering of nonlinear dynamics in graphene resonators and provide a general strategy for active control of the mechanical response of two-dimensional nanoelectromechanical systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The research investigates why colloidal quantum dots exhibit unstable cathodoluminescence under electron beams. They demonstrate that this instability stems from detrimental electron-beam-induced charging, which drives multiexciton formation. By replacing long surface ligands with shorter ones, the researchers successfully improve charge drainage and stabilize the emission process for practical device applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an experimental demonstration of controlling many-body dynamics (biexciton generation) in nanoscale emitters by manipulating the charge environment, which is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems and charge transport in solid-state nanodevices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is understanding and stabilizing the unstable Cathodoluminescence (CL) response of Colloidal Quantum Dots (QDs) when excited by an electron beam.

**Main result.** The CL instability, blueshift, and bleaching are shown to be governed by electron-beam-induced charging, which can be mitigated by using shorter surface ligands to improve charge drainage.

**Method.** In situ measurements combining Cathodoluminescence (CL) and Photoluminescence (PL) spectroscopy, alongside ligand exchange experiments, were used to probe excited state dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system involves giant-shell CdSe/CdS colloidal quantum dots. The analysis distinguishes between neutral excitons ($	ext{X}_0$), charged excitons ($	ext{X}^*$), and biexcitons ($	ext{XX}$) using spectral fitting.

**Key observables.** Spectral evolution (blueshift), decay lifetimes, relative contributions of $	ext{X}^0$, $	ext{X}^*$, and $	ext{XX}$ components, and intensity degradation (cathodobleaching).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron beam current (from high to sub-pA regimes), ligand length (long oleate vs. short MPA), and excitation voltage.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes that charge accumulation is the central mechanism driving the observed instability in the CL response, rather than solely local heating or surface modification.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate setup schematics, TEM images of QDs, spectral evolution (blueshift) and intensity degradation under electron beam exposure, and comparisons between PL/CL spectra before and after ligand treatment.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the instability problem using CL/PL measurements, identifying charge accumulation as the cause via current dependence studies, and finally proposing a solution through surface chemistry modification (ligand exchange) to control multi-exciton formation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Colloidal quantum dots (QDs) are attractive nanoscale emitters, yet their cathodoluminescence (CL) response remains poorly understood and often unstable under electron-beam excitation, limiting CL spectroscopy and electron-beam-based device processing. Here, we investigate the CL mechanism and strategies to improve its stability using highly photostable, structurally homogeneous giant-shell CdSe/CdS QDs combined with in situ CL and photoluminescence (PL) measurements. By identifying distinct signatures of excited states in both lifetime and spectral measurements, we demonstrate that the CL response is governed by electron-beam-induced charging. Charge accumulation drives multiexciton generation even at relatively low currents, leading to a pronounced blueshift, shorter average lifetimes, and rapid cathodobleaching. To test this further, we employ indirect excitation to reach sub-pA currents beyond the limits of typical electron beams, showing that neutral-exciton emission can be partially recovered and cathodobleaching mitigated, although charging cannot be fully suppressed. Furthermore, by replacing long insulating ligands with shorter ones, we improve charge drainage and strongly suppress biexciton formation. Together, these results show that biexciton formation can be controlled by limiting charge accumulation, providing a practical route toward stable CL for spectroscopy, imaging, and electron-beam-compatible photonic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper advocates for a paradigm shift in studying magnetic nanoparticles, treating inherent structural disorder not as an imperfection but as an engineerable resource. By advancing micromagnetic simulations and coupling them with experimental data from SANS, the authors aim to establish quantitative rules linking particle structure, internal magnetization textures, and desired macroscopic performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical magnetism, the methodology—using advanced simulations (micromagnetics) constrained by ensemble measurements (SANS) to map structure-to-function relationships in disordered media—is highly relevant for understanding complex many-body dynamics in open quantum systems or spin glasses.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The field needs to move beyond treating spin disorder in magnetic nanoparticles as a mere defect, instead engineering this disorder for enhanced macroscopic functionality.

**Main result.** Disorder can generate qualitatively new macroscopic behaviors (e.g., non-monotonic coercivity) that are inaccessible when assuming perfect or simple uniform structures.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical modeling using micromagnetics with experimental constraints derived from techniques like Polarized Small-Angle Neutron Scattering (SANS).

**Model / system.** The system is magnetic nanoparticles, often modeled as iron oxide nanoflowers exhibiting polycrystalline intraparticle disorder. The model progresses from the simplified macrospin approximation to detailed micromagnetic simulations.

**Key observables.** Coercivity ($\mu_0 H_C$), ensemble-averaged magnetic correlations (measured by SANS), and macroscopic functionality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree of intergrain exchange coupling ($k$) is a key parameter controlling the transition between different magnetic reversal regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** A major assumption/limitation discussed is that current models struggle to uniquely decouple the roles of structural disorder, anisotropy variation, and exchange coupling using only macroscopic measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare macrospin vs. micromagnetism results and show coercivity ($\mu_0 H_C$) dependence on the coupling parameter $k$, illustrating distinct reversal pathways (vortex-core vs. flux-closure).

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews the concept of disorder engineering, critiques limitations of the macrospin model, proposes micromagnetics as a necessary tool to resolve intra-particle textures, and outlines future directions toward inverse design using SANS constraints.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin disorder, inherent to magnetic nanoparticles, has traditionally been regarded as a detrimental feature, with materials-engineering efforts largely focused on producing ''perfect particles'' containing as few defects as possible. Alongside this pursuit of perfection, however, an alternative framework has emerged in recent years that reframes intra-particle disorder as an ''ugly duckling'' whose functional potential remains to be unlocked. In this Perspective, we review the emerging concept of disorder engineering in magnetic nanoparticles, identify its current challenges, and outline promising future directions. From a theoretical standpoint, progress requires moving beyond the widely used macrospin approximation, which severely restricts the description of intra-particle degrees of freedom. Micromagnetic modelling, in contrast, treats magnetisation as a continuous vector field and thereby enables (i) the explicit representation of intra-particle degrees of freedom, linking microstructural features to internal magnetisation textures, and (ii) direct correspondence with polarized small-angle neutron scattering, an experimental technique that provides quantitative access to ensemble-averaged magnetic correlations on nanometre length scales. The field must now advance towards falsifiable and uncertainty-aware models with structurally motivated parameters and predictions that can be tested against independent experimental observables. The overarching goal is to establish quantitative relationships between particle structure, intra-particle magnetisation textures, and macroscopic functionality, thereby transforming spin disorder from an elusive hidden variable into an engineerable design parameter.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses advanced pulsed nanocalorimetry to study magnetic ordering in atomically thin CrSBr flakes. By measuring heat capacity and entropy across varying thicknesses and fields, the researchers map out a dimensional crossover from bulk interlayer antiferromagnetism to intralayer ferromagnetism. The findings establish nanocalorimetry as a powerful tool for probing magnetic entropy in 2D quantum magnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the use of nanocalorimetry to extract entropy changes ($\Delta S$) provides a direct, quantitative measure of phase transitions and correlation energy scales in low-dimensional systems, which is highly relevant for understanding quantum many-body ground states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how magnetic order evolves in two-dimensional (2D) van der Waals magnets as their thickness is reduced toward the monolayer limit.

**Main result.** The research reveals a crossover from bulk interlayer antiferromagnetism to intralayer ferromagnetic correlations, accompanied by a pronounced layer-parity effect and thickness-dependent suppression of magnetic order under an applied field.

**Method.** Microsecond pulse-heating nanocalorimetry is employed to measure the heat capacity and associated magnetic entropy changes in exfoliated flakes.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Chromium sulfide bromide (CrSBr), a van der Waals magnet exhibiting A-type antiferromagnetism. Measurements are performed on flakes ranging from several layers down to the monolayer limit, often under an applied in-plane magnetic field.

**Key observables.** Heat capacity anomaly ($\Delta C_p/T$), magnetic entropy release ($\Delta S(T)$), and thickness-dependent critical fields for antiferromagnetic order suppression.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Layer number (ML to monolayer limit), applied in-plane magnetic field ($H$), and temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on subtracting background contributions, including phonon contributions modeled by a Debye function, from the total heat capacity measurement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $C_p(T)$ vs. thickness showing transition anomalies; field dependence plots show suppression of interlayer transitions; and supplementary figures detail background subtraction procedures.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents measurements across varying thicknesses and magnetic fields, analyzing the resulting thermodynamic anomalies to map out the crossover between different types of magnetic correlations (interlayer vs. intralayer).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding the evolution of magnetic order in the two-dimensional limit remains a central challenge in van der Waals magnets, where thermodynamic measurements are constrained by the femtogram-scale mass of exfoliated flakes. Here, we use microsecond pulse-heating nanocalorimetry to measure the heat capacity and magnetic entropy of CrSBr flakes down to the monolayer limit. The measurements reveal the entropy landscape associated with magnetic ordering, uncovering a reduction of the interlayer transition temperature and an entropy-derived effective magnetic moment approaching the monolayer limit. Thermodynamic anomalies capture a crossover from bulk-like interlayer antiferromagnetism to a regime dominated by intralayer ferromagnetic correlations. A pronounced layer-parity effect further emerges, with odd-layer samples displaying an additional high-temperature contribution associated with uncompensated magnetic layers. Under in-plane magnetic fields applied along the easy axis, antiferromagnetic order is progressively suppressed, allowing extraction of a thickness-dependent critical field reflecting weakened interlayer exchange coupling. Entropy analysis further reveals an extended regime of magnetic fluctuations persisting well above the interlayer ordering transition. Together, these results establish nanocalorimetry as a powerful thermodynamic probe of low-dimensional magnetism, providing direct access to magnetic entropy, exchange interactions, and dimensional crossover in atomically thin van der Waals magnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a rigorous theoretical framework using boundary value problems to analyze nonlinear microwave propagation in superconducting HKI transmission lines. By retaining full spatial field dependence, the authors predict strong directionality (diode effect) and complex spectral features like bistability and anti-resonance shifts purely from structural asymmetry. This provides a versatile tool for designing next-generation nonlinear superconducting microwave components.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconducting circuits, the methodology—developing a robust BVP approach for strongly nonlinear wave equations with spatial dependence—is highly relevant to modeling non-linear dynamics in open quantum systems or photonic structures where boundary conditions are critical.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a theoretical framework for nonlinear wave propagation in High-Kinetic-Inductance (HKI) transmission lines that remains accurate even when spatial field variations are strong, overcoming the limitations of the slowly varying envelope approximation.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated power-dependent resonance shifts, bistable solutions, and significant direction-dependent transport (diode effect) in superconducting microwave resonators without external magnetic biasing fields.

**Method.** The problem is formulated as a boundary value problem (BVP), allowing for the retention of full spatial field dependence, which is solved using advanced numerical techniques like the shooting method.

**Model / system.** The system involves nonlinear HKI superconducting microwave resonators modeled by incorporating a nonlinear inductance per unit length into the telegrapher's equations. The analysis extends to multi-segment and stub geometries.

**Key observables.** Power-dependent resonance shifts, bistable transmission solutions, direction-dependent transport contrast (up to 93%), and Duffing-like spectral distortions in anti-resonances.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The nonlinearity is governed by the kinetic inductance term; key regimes include moderate power ($P \ll Z I_*^2$) and structural asymmetry between impedance segments ($Z_1 
eq Z_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial analysis uses a harmonic balance ansatz, assuming higher harmonics are negligible, although the full BVP approach mitigates this limitation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic layouts of nonlinear resonators and stub geometries; spectral plots compare $S_{21}$ vs $S_{12}$ to show directionality, and power response curves demonstrate bistability.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the necessity of a full spatial boundary value problem over envelope approximations. It then applies this framework to analyze two-port scattering (showing diode effects) and extends it to multi-segment and stub geometries to explore complex nonlinear interference patterns.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-kinetic-inductance (HKI) transmission lines provide a promising platform for compact nonlinear superconducting microwave devices. Existing theoretical descriptions are typically based on the slowly varying envelope approximation, which becomes inadequate for strongly nonlinear resonant structures with pronounced spatial field variations. Here, we develop a theoretical framework for single-frequency nonlinear wave propagation in HKI transmission lines by formulating the problem as a boundary value problem that retains the full spatial dependence of the electromagnetic fields. Applying the method to resonant transmission-line geometries, we demonstrate power-dependent resonance shifts, bistable transmission solutions, and strongly direction-dependent transport arising from asymmetric impedance barriers, yielding transmission contrasts of up to $93\%$ without magnetic bias fields. The framework is further extended to a three-port stub geometry, where nonlinear interference produces shifted anti-resonances and Duffing-like spectral distortions. Our approach provides a versatile tool for the analysis and design of strongly nonlinear superconducting microwave devices.

</details>

### [Nonlinear Meissner States, Vortex Sheets, and Laminar Structures in Extreme Type-II Superconductors](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29608v1)

**Authors:** Eugene B. Kolomeisky  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.29608v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Frenkel-Kontorova` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **2/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

<img src="2607.29608_figures/2607.29608_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. The velocity v, magnetic induction b and super- conducting density ρ in the soliton (8). Inset: Construction of Meissner states by truncating the universal soliton at the vacuum-superconductor boundary x = xs.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes exotic magnetic structures, such as vortex sheets and laminar lattices, within extreme Type-II superconductors using a nonlinear velocity theory. The work derives exact solutions demonstrating how the superconducting state evolves from a Meissner profile through soliton-like defects into periodic mixed states. It provides a detailed mathematical description of these fundamental phase transitions in high-field superconductivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity, the mathematical techniques—solving nonlinear partial differential equations to find stable topological defects (solitons) and phase transitions between ordered states (Meissner $	o$ Vortex Lattice)—are highly relevant to understanding dynamics in other complex physical systems studied in AMO or quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates nonlinear Meissner states, vortex sheets, and laminar structures that emerge in extreme Type-II superconductors.

**Main result.** It derives exact solutions showing that the superconducting state transitions through soliton-like vortex sheets into periodic laminar mixed states as the applied field increases.

**Method.** The analysis uses a nonlinear velocity theory derived from Ginzburg-Landau theory, focusing on solving the resulting one-dimensional transverse free energy functional.

**Model / system.** The system is extreme Type-II superconductors ($\kappa 	o \infty$), modeled using a nonlinear velocity theory where the free energy depends only on the superfluid velocity $v$. The governing equation simplifies to Ampère's law in terms of $v$ and the magnetic induction $b$.

**Key observables.** Nonlinear Meissner states, vortex sheets (solitons), laminar structures (periodic solutions), and the thermodynamic critical field ($h_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The penetration depth parameter $\kappa$, applied external magnetic field $h$, and characteristic fields like $h_{c1}$ and $h_c$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes the extreme Type-II limit ($\kappa 	o \infty$) allows for a simplification of the Ginzburg-Landau functional to depend only on velocity $v$ in the 1D transverse sector.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the profiles ($v, b, ho$) associated with the soliton solution and its truncation defining Meissner states.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the nonlinear theory by analyzing the free energy functional, deriving the governing differential equation, and then solving for specific structures: the Meissner state (half-soliton), the vortex sheet (static soliton), and periodic solutions representing laminar lattices.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A recently derived nonlinear velocity theory of extreme type-II superconductors is shown to possess an exactly solvable one-dimensional sector. Its solutions include nonlinear Meissner states exhibiting universal tails, vortex sheets, and periodic laminar structures. The thermodynamic critical field emerges from a marginal Meissner profile equivalent to the normal-superconducting boundary and may be viewed as a half-soliton. The vortex sheet is a soliton characterized by a discontinuity of the velocity field, a continuous and localized magnetic field and vanishing superconducting density at its center, and may be interpreted as the coarse-grained limit of a dense row of Abrikosov vortices. Periodic solutions describe laminar states that may be interpreted as coarse-grained rectangular vortex lattices.

</details>

**Authors:** David Hernández-Pinilla, Line Jelver, María Jesús Martínez-Morillo, César Hernando-Fuente, Miquel Cherta, Guillermo López-Polin, Joel D. Cox, Luisa E. Bausá, Mariola O Ramírez

**Summary.** This study reports on the domain-selective control of Second Harmonic Generation in MoS2 integrated with a ferroelectric LiNbO3 substrate. By exploiting the polarization dependence of the underlying ferroelectric domains, researchers induce photodoping in the MoS2 monolayer. This doping significantly enhances the material's nonlinear susceptibility ($\chi^{(2)}$), enabling programmable and switchable frequency conversion for advanced photonic applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a clear demonstration of using built-in electric fields (ferroelectricity) combined with external optical pumping (photodoping) as an active knob to engineer the nonlinear optical properties of 2D materials, which is highly relevant for advanced quantum light sources and modulators.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to actively control the nonlinear optical response, specifically Second Harmonic Generation (SHG), in monolayer MoS2 by leveraging the ferroelectric domain pattern of an underlying substrate.

**Main result.** Ferroelectric polarization-controlled photodoping at the MoS2/LiNbO3 interface enhances the effective second-order susceptibility ($\chi^{(2)}$), leading to a significant, up to ~70%, enhancement in SHG intensity between domains of opposite polarization.

**Method.** The research combines all-optical, spatially resolved SHG imaging experiments with ab initio Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations to model the electronic structure and $\chi^{(2)}$ dependence on doping.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a hybrid heterostructure combining monolayer molybdenum disulfide (MoS2) with periodically poled lithium niobate (LiNbO3). The ferroelectric domains in LiNbO3 induce localized photodoping in the MoS2, which modulates its electronic band structure and nonlinear optical response.

**Key observables.** SHG intensity modulation correlated with ferroelectric domain polarization ($	ext{P}_{	ext{up}}$ vs. $	ext{P}_{	ext{down}}$), effective second-order susceptibility ($\chi^{(2)}$), and the dependence of SHG on excitation wavelength and fundamental beam power.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The enhancement magnitude (~70%), the role of carrier density (photodoping level, e.g., $\sim 5	imes 10^{14} 	ext{ cm}^{-2}$), and the resonant excitation conditions near the C interband transition.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the ferroelectric polarization dictates the local charge doping in MoS2, which in turn governs the magnitude of $\chi^{(2)}$ via band structure modification.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spatial SHG mapping showing domain-dependent contrast; spectral analysis shows enhancement near two-photon resonance; and theoretical figures map calculated $\chi^{(2)}$ values based on simulated doping levels.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the platform (MoS2/LiNbO3), presents experimental measurements demonstrating polarization-dependent SHG modulation, attributes this to light-induced photodoping via domain control, and supports these findings with DFT calculations linking doping to $\chi^{(2)}$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hybrid heterostructures combining two-dimensional semiconductors with ferroelectric materials offer a versatile route to actively control light-matter interactions at the nanoscale. Here, we report all-optical, light-induced domain-selective control of second-harmonic generation (SHG) in monolayer molybdenum disulfide (MoS$_2$) integrated with periodically poled lithium niobate (LiNbO$_3$). Spatially resolved SHG imaging reveals a pronounced modulation of the nonlinear optical response of monolayer MoS$_2$ governed by the ferroelectric domain pattern of the underlying substrate. A strong SHG contrast is observed between domains of opposite polarization, with a marked dependence on both the excitation wavelength and the incident optical power. The comparison between ferroelectric domains that either enable or do not exhibit light-driven photodoping in the MoS$_2$ monolayer provides a direct assessment of the role of carrier density in the nonlinear optical response. We find that ferroelectric-polarization-controlled photodoping at the MoS$_2$/LiNbO$_3$ interface enhances the effective second-order susceptibility, $χ^2$, producing an increase in SHG intensity of up to ~70% under resonant excitation conditions. Ab initio calculations corroborate that charge doping modifies the electronic band structure of MoS$_2$ and strongly affects $χ^2$ in the resonant regime, providing microscopic support for the experimentally observed modulation. The results highlight the combination of light intensity and ferroelectricity as a powerful knob for band-structure modulation in 2D materials and reconfigurable nonlinear optical responses, opening pathways toward programmable frequency conversion, smart light modulators, and advanced nonlinear photonic functionalities in integrated hybrid platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates when electronic Poiseuille flow in 2D materials becomes unstable using linear stability theory. By solving a modified Orr-Sommerfeld equation, the authors determine the minimum current density required to induce turbulence. The findings pinpoint optimal geometric conditions for observing this fundamental fluid dynamic instability in nanoscale electronics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluid dynamics, the mathematical framework of analyzing instabilities via linearized partial differential equations is highly relevant to open quantum systems and non-equilibrium dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study analyzes the linear stability of electronic Poiseuille flow within two-dimensional materials.

**Main result.** The instability onset is characterized by a minimum critical current density required for turbulence, which occurs at specific geometric ratios ($W \approx 2D$).

**Method.** Linear stability analysis using a modified Orr-Sommerfeld equation derived from the Navier-Stokes equations.

**Model / system.** The system models electron flow in a 2D material (like graphene) under plane Poiseuille flow, governed by continuity and a modified Navier-Stokes equation incorporating electrical driving forces.

**Key observables.** Critical current density ($J_{\min}$), critical Reynolds number ($	ext{Re}_c$), streamwise wavenumber ($k_c$), and frequency ($f_{cr}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Channel width to diffusion length ratio ($W/D$) and boundary scattering length ($\ell_B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes linear perturbations, neglecting non-linear terms; it also uses simplified models for electron interactions (e.g., assuming $\ell_{ee}$ is small).

**Figures summary.** Figures show velocity profiles for varying geometric ratios and stability diagrams mapping the transition from stable to unstable flow regions as a function of current density.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the governing equations, performs linear stability analysis by solving an eigenvalue problem (modified Orr-Sommerfeld equation), and analyzes results in various limiting regimes ($W/D$ ratios).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Motivated by the experimental observation of electronic Poiseuille flow in graphene [J.A. Sulpizio et al. Nature 576, 75 (2019)] we analyze the linear stability of plane-Poiseuille flow of electrons in a two-dimensional material using a modified Orr-Sommerfeld equation. We calculate the critical current needed to make the flow unstable as a function of the experimental parameters and characterize the most favorable situation, that is the one needing the lowest current density, to observe flow instability. We predict the streamwise wavenumber and frequency at which the instability occurs and discuss the difficulties of an experiment aimed at probing this phenomenon.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper establishes a group-theoretical framework to analyze topological properties like Berry phase and BICs in 2D Photonic Crystals. By adapting solid-state IR formalism, it systematically maps how crystal symmetries dictate mode behavior. This provides a powerful tool for designing photonic structures with tailored topological responses.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful mathematical tool (group theory) to analyze wave phenomena in engineered optical structures, bridging solid-state topology concepts directly into the realm of photonics and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a group-theoretical framework for understanding local k-space topology and Berry phase in 2D Photonic Crystals (PhCs).

**Main result.** The irreducible-representation formalism allows for the systematic classification of topological phenomena, showing that non-Abelian groups are key for Berry phase emergence.

**Method.** Applying group theory concepts, specifically decomposing basis functions using symmetry operations and block-diagonalizing the Bloch Hamiltonian to analyze mode coupling.

**Model / system.** The system is 2D Photonic Crystals (PhCs), modeled by a Bloch Hamiltonian adapted from solid-state physics, incorporating radiative coupling and transverse conditions. The analysis extends beyond scalar Schrödinger operators.

**Key observables.** Local k-space topology, Berry phase, symmetry-protected degeneracies, BIC linewidths, and mode hybridization conditions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** High symmetry points ($\Gamma$-point), structural design parameters, and coefficients associated with targeted irreducible representations (IRs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system can be effectively decoupled from the radiation continuum for initial block decomposition, though extensions are discussed.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows representative site geometries in real space for square and triangular lattices, annotated with little groups at high-symmetry points.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the IR formalism to classify topology, applies it to study BICs/vortex beams using PWE and non-Hermitian Hamiltonians, and concludes by detailing how structural or gain/loss perturbations can systematically manipulate these topological features.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-dimensional photonic crystals (2D PhCs) enable fine-grained control over a broad set of Bloch modes without the constraints of band occupancy and natural crystal structures, and, as intrinsically open systems, serve as versatile platforms for exploring diverse topological phenomena. Here, we develop a theoretical framework inspired by the irreducible-representation formalism in solid-state physics, while explicitly incorporating key characteristics of photonic Bloch systems, such as radiative coupling and transverse condition. Within this framework, we study the symmetry origins of local k-space topology, e.g., bound-states in the continuum and optical vortex beams, and Berry phase in two representative systems. We further analyze, from a group-theory perspective, how tailored structural designs and targeted symmetry perturbations can be exploited to manipulate these topological features. In particular, we showcase the application of the formalism to Bloch modes from distinct truncation approaches and specify the preferable regimes for each, both under a generic $n$-band configuration. The analysis can thereby be readily extended to a wide range of artificial wave crystals beyond scalar Schrödinger-like operators and two-level treatment.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on advanced experiments measuring Coulomb drag in 1D semiconductor quantum wires. By systematically varying temperature and magnetic field, the authors resolved a transition between two distinct physical regimes: one dominated by momentum conservation and another averaged over disorder. This establishes a quantitative method for characterizing electron-electron interactions in these complex low-dimensional systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly quantitative experimental framework for probing fundamental many-body interactions (like those in TLLs) in low dimensions by using transport measurements sensitive to both quantum confinement effects (B-field oscillations) and thermal scattering mechanisms (T-dependence).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the challenge of understanding electron-electron interactions in one-dimensional (1D) systems, particularly for Tomonaga-Luttinger liquids, complicated by disorder effects observed in Coulomb drag experiments.

**Main result.** The researchers successfully characterized 1D interaction parameters and identified two distinct temperature regimes—momentum-resolved and disordered-averaged—by analyzing the systematic evolution of the Coulomb drag signal with magnetic field and temperature.

**Method.** Measurements involve performing Coulomb drag measurements (measuring induced voltage/resistance) in vertically coupled quantum wires while varying perpendicular magnetic fields and temperatures, complemented by magnetic depopulation analysis.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a system of vertically coupled GaAs-AlGaAs quantum wires acting as drive and drag conductors. The physics involves electron transport in quasi-1D Luttinger liquid regimes subjected to external magnetic fields and temperature variations.

**Key observables.** Coulomb drag voltage/resistance ($R_{Drag}$), magnetoconductance oscillations, and the crossover temperature ($T_1$) separating different scaling regimes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (T), perpendicular Magnetic Field (B), Luttinger liquid interaction parameters (K), and disorder strength effects.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed drag signal is dominated by momentum transfer when subbands align, and it models the transition between different scattering/relaxation regimes based on temperature scaling laws (power-law vs. Arrhenius).

**Figures summary.** Figures show 2D maps of $R_{Drag}$ dependence on gate voltage and B, demonstrating oscillatory behavior tracking magnetic depopulation; plots illustrate the systematic shift and suppression of drag peaks with increasing B, and fit results confirm power-law (Mid-T) and Arrhenius (High-T) temperature dependencies.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the experimental setup using quantum wires, details measurements across varying T and B fields to observe subband depopulation via magnetoconductance, analyzes $R_{Drag}$ oscillations tracking these depopulations, and finally maps out the temperature dependence to define the crossover between momentum-resolved and disorder-averaged drag regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Advancing the understanding of electron-electron interactions in one-dimensional systems remains one of the central challenges in low-dimensional physics, especially for Coulomb-coupled Tomonaga-Luttinger liquids. Notably, the difficulty of reliably extracting one-dimensional system parameters, combined with the presence of disorder, has hindered the interpretation of 1D Coulomb drag experiments. Here, we present a self-consistent experimental determination of the relative Luttinger liquid interaction parameters through 1D Coulomb drag measurements, and achieve quantitative agreement with theoretical predictions. Utilizing vertically coupled GaAs-AlGaAs quantum wires, we fully characterize the one-dimensional parameters through magnetic depopulation. Coulomb drag exhibits a systematic evolution with magnetic field, reflecting the successive depopulation of 1D subbands and the suppression of disorder effects. Two distinct temperature regimes are identified, marking the boundary between momentum-resolved and disordered-averaged Coulomb drag. The observed scaling, peak broadening, and nonlinear current-voltage characteristics establish a unified and quantitative framework for probing electron-electron interactions in 1D systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper demonstrates that exciton properties are highly sensitive to the quantum geometry of Bloch states, especially near topological phase transitions. By analyzing a Lieb-lattice model, the authors show that the momentum dependence of electron and hole wavefunctions suppresses Coulomb interactions, which in turn enlarges the real-space exciton radius. This effect provides an experimentally accessible signature—an enhanced diamagnetic response—that relies on quantum geometry rather than just semiclassical parameters.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete mechanism linking topological band structure features (like Berry curvature) directly to measurable optical properties (exciton size), offering an advanced route for material design beyond simple effective mass theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum geometry, specifically near a topological band-gap closing, fundamentally alters exciton properties like their size.

**Main result.** Suppression of the electron-hole Coulomb matrix elements due to momentum-dependent Bloch states leads to a narrowing in momentum space and a measurable enlargement of the exciton radius in real space.

**Method.** The authors solve the band-projected Wannier equation using a Lieb-lattice model, comparing results obtained with full quantum geometry effects versus simplified unity-overlap approximations.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a spin–orbit-coupled Lieb-lattice Hamiltonian tuned toward a topological phase transition. Excitons are treated as fundamental excitations whose properties depend on the Bloch states of the underlying electron and hole bands.

**Key observables.** Exciton radius ($\xi$), weak-field diamagnetic response, projected Coulomb matrix elements ($|\Lambda_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}'}|$), and the Hilbert–Schmidt quantum distance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Topological phase transition point (band-gap closing), spin-orbit coupling strength ($\lambda_{SOC}$), and momentum separation ($q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on defining the exciton state via a projected Wannier equation and utilizing covariant derivatives to incorporate Berry connections into the position operator.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate single-particle band structures, Berry curvature distribution across varying SOC strengths, and plots showing the suppression of the form factor $|e\Lambda_{q_{	ext{ref}}, q}|$ as a function of momentum angle $	heta$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the theoretical framework by defining the projected relative-position operator using covariant derivatives. It then applies this to a Lieb lattice, showing how topological transitions modify the quantum metric and subsequently alter exciton size and diamagnetic response compared to semiclassical limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exciton engineering traditionally focuses on modifying semiclassical material properties, such as the effective mass and dielectric screening, while largely overlooking the quantum geometry of the underlying electron and hole Bloch states. This approximation is adequate for many materials but breaks down near a topological band-gap closing, where the quantum metric around the band extrema becomes strongly enhanced. In this regime, Bloch states at different momenta become less similar, reducing the projected electron--hole Coulomb matrix elements and consequently weakening exciton binding. We demonstrate this mechanism in a spin--orbit-coupled Lieb-lattice model tuned toward a topological phase transition. The suppressed Coulomb matrix elements narrow the exciton wavefunction in momentum space, leading to an enlarged exciton radius in real space. This increase in exciton size produces an experimentally accessible enhancement of the weak-field diamagnetic response. Our results show that quantum geometry can fundamentally reshape exciton properties near a topological phase transition, revealing a previously underexplored route for engineering excitonic states.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the intrinsic Orbital Hall Effect in spin-3/2 degenerate semiconductors. It proves that this effect is not due to the Berry curvature, but rather arises purely from interband contributions mediated by the quantum metric. This establishes a distinct, geometrically controlled mechanism for orbital transport.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the reliance on 'quantum geometry' (the quantum metric) as a primary physical observable connects directly to geometric phases and topological invariants studied in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the intrinsic Orbital Hall Effect (OHE) in degenerate spin-3/2 systems to determine the fundamental geometric quantity governing this transport.

**Main result.** The OHE is found to be governed entirely by the quantum metric acting on interband orbital magnetic moment components, rather than the Berry curvature or conventional intraband contributions.

**Method.** Theoretical calculation using linear response theory applied to a Luttinger Hamiltonian model, focusing on matrix elements of the Orbital Magnetic Moment (OMM).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as degenerate spin-3/2 semiconductors described by a Luttinger Hamiltonian. The analysis leverages symmetries inherent in this degenerate manifold.

**Key observables.** Orbital Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{z,	ext{Hall}}^{yx}$), Orbital Magnetic Moment (OMM), Quantum Metric components ($g_{xx}, g_{yx}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fermi energy ($\epsilon_F$), material parameters related to heavy-hole and light-hole band gaps ($\gamma_{3/2} - \gamma_{1/2}$), and the Luttinger Hamiltonian coefficients.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes rotational invariance, utilizes the Luttinger model framework, and relies on approximations like the spherical approximation for tractability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic dispersion relations comparing linear and quadratic Hamiltonians, showing where intraband OMM vanishes in the degenerate spectrum. Contour plots visualize momentum-space distributions of the quantum metric components.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem by noting that conventional contributions vanish in degenerate systems. It then develops the theory to show the survival of an interband mechanism governed solely by the quantum metric, culminating in explicit analytical expressions and numerical estimates for various semiconductors.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that in rotationally invariant spin-$3/2$ systems the orbital Hall effect originates from interband matrix elements of the orbital magnetic moment. The resulting Hall response is governed by the quantum metric, rather than by the Berry curvature, revealing a purely geometric transport mechanism. Conventional intraband contributions associated with the orbital magnetic moment and Berry curvature are shown to vanish identically in degenerate systems. These results identify the quantum metric as the key geometric quantity controlling orbital Hall transport in degenerate multiband systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work addresses the challenge of predicting properties in disordered High-Entropy Perovskite Oxides by using graph neural networks trained via transfer learning. The study finds that while structural stability ($	ext{E}_f$) transfers well, electronic gaps ($	ext{E}_g$) require targeted fine-tuning with HEPO data. It highlights that incorporating local geometric information is crucial for accurate modeling of complex disordered materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state materials, the core methodology—using advanced ML (GNNs) to extrapolate physical laws from a known, ordered regime to a highly complex, disordered one—is directly relevant to modeling many-body systems where disorder or quenched randomness is present.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Accurately predicting material properties, specifically formation energy and HOMO-LUMO gap, in chemically complex and structurally disordered High-Entropy Perovskite Oxides (HEPOs).

**Main result.** Knowledge transfer from ordered perovskites is effective for formation energy but fails for the HOMO-LUMO gap due to local environment sensitivity; incorporating small HEPO data significantly improves electronic property prediction.

**Method.** The study employs graph neural networks (GNNs) trained using an ordered-to-disordered transfer learning paradigm, comparing performance across four different GNN architectures.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the perovskite oxide structure ($	ext{ABO}_3$), modeled computationally using Special Quasirandom Structures (SQSs) to represent chemical and structural disorder in HEPOs. The prediction targets are DFT-calculated formation energy ($E_f$) and HOMO-LUMO gap ($E_g$).

**Key observables.** Formation energy ($	ext{E}_f$), HOMO-LUMO gap ($	ext{E}_g$), Mean Absolute Error (MAE), and residual distributions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The transferability of knowledge, the fraction of HEPO-specific training data used for fine-tuning, and the inclusion of three-body geometric information (e.g., bond angles).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Knowledge learned from chemically ordered perovskites can serve as a starting point for predicting properties in disordered HEPOs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate training loss trajectories showing ALIGNN's superior convergence, and MAE comparisons demonstrating the performance of different models on both ordered and HEPO test sets.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the challenge posed by HEPOs. It then details the methodology using GNNs for transfer learning from ordered to disordered domains. Results are presented by comparing $	ext{E}_f$ vs. $	ext{E}_g$ transferability, followed by an analysis of model performance and optimal data incorporation strategies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-entropy perovskite oxides (HEPOs) represent a chemically complex class of materials with promising functional properties, yet their vast compositional space and, chemical/structural disorder pose significant challenge for accurate property prediction. Graph neural networks (GNNs) enable rapid exploration of materials space but are often limited by the availability of representative training data. Here, we investigate ordered-to-disordered transfer learning using GNNs for formation-energy and HOMO-LUMO gap prediction in HEPOs by transferring knowledge learned from chemically ordered perovskites. Four representative GNN models, including CGCNN, GATGNN, ALIGNN and M3GNet are evaluated to understand the role of structural representations, spanning pairwise two-body and angular three-body interactions in transfer performance. We find strong property-dependent transfer behavior: formation-energy prediction transfers effectively to disordered HEPOs, whereas HOMO-LUMO gap prediction shows limited transferability due to its sensitivity to local chemical environments. Incorporating a small HEPO-specific training dataset substantially improves HOMO-LUMO gap prediction. Representation-level analysis using UMAP further highlights the importance of encoding three-body geometric information such as in ALIGNN for capturing complex structure-property relationships and improving transferability.

</details>

(a) At γ3=0, four lobes of alternating sign whose net response vanishes, ⟨I⟩= 0, the emergent-C∞symmetry cancellation. (b) With γ3 the release is a small distortion of a large, near-cancelling background. (c) Their difference, the O(γ3) released component, carries a nonzero net scalar concentrated near K. (d) The same released component at the time-reversed valley K′ [HK′(q) = HK(−q)∗]: the q →−q image of (c); the two valleys add rather than cancel, consistent with the mirror-even σyyy.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work derives a symmetry selection rule that explains the sign reversal of band-edge shift currents in 2D Dirac systems like graphene. By utilizing a three-point Bargmann invariant and analyzing emergent rotational symmetries, the authors show that warping breaks a protective symmetry, enabling a gate-switchable photovoltaic response.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous derivation of an exact angular selection rule governing a measurable photocurrent response provides deep insight into how crystal symmetries dictate electronic transport properties in low-dimensional materials, relevant for understanding topological or strongly correlated responses.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the sign reversal of large band-edge shift currents observed in graphene multilayers, a phenomenon not explained by standard quantum metrics like Berry curvature.

**Main result.** It establishes an exact angular selection rule showing that an emergent rotational symmetry forbids the current in 2D, and this response is switched on linearly by trigonal warping. This makes the current parameter-free and gate-switchable in bilayer/trilayer graphene.

**Method.** The analysis uses advanced concepts from quantum geometry, developing a three-point Bargmann invariant to characterize the bulk photovoltaic response ($\sigma_{a;bc}(\omega)$).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on inversion-broken, two-dimensional gapped Dirac-like systems, specifically modeling graphene multilayers and the kagome lattice.

**Key observables.** Shift current ($	ext{SC}$), three-point Bargmann invariant ($T^{nmp}$), and the bounded coherence factor ($C$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate voltage (for sign reversal), warping strength, and dimensionality (2D vs 3D).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the bulk, second-order-in-field mechanism near band-edge touchings.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the microscopic shift current contribution ($\propto |r_b^{nm}|^2 \mathbf{R}_{a;b}^{nm}$) and depict the closed loop structure of the three-point Bargmann triangle in band space.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from identifying the unexplained sign reversal to formulating the response using a three-point Bargmann invariant. It then derives the symmetry selection rule, showing the prohibition in 2D due to $	ext{SO}(2)$ symmetry and its restoration by $C_3$ warping.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The shift current is the intrinsic bulk photovoltaic response of a crystal without an inversion center. In graphene multilayers, recent calculations report large band-edge shift currents that reverse sign under a gate or displacement field, a behavior that neither the quantum metric nor the Berry curvature captures. We show that this behavior follows from a symmetry principle: at the absorption edge of an inversion-broken, two-dimensional gapped Dirac-like system, an emergent low-energy rotational symmetry forbids the response, and the trigonal warping, which reduces this emergent symmetry to the lattice's three-fold rotation, switches the current on linearly in its strength. We formulate this as an exact angular selection rule that unifies the band-edge responses of multilayer graphene and of the kagome lattice. In the multiband structures, the released current is governed by a signed, detuning-weighted three-point Bargmann invariant of the Bloch states: the optical amplitude remains fixed while the sign alignment of the Bargmann triangles grows with the warping, a phase-coherence effect captured by a bounded coherence factor and invisible to any positive-definite figure of merit, the quantum metric included. In bilayer and trilayer graphene, a gate voltage alone drives the sign reversal, making the band-edge shift current a parameter-free, gate-switchable bulk photovoltaic response.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically explores achieving Chern insulating behavior in 2D lattices by manipulating orbital couplings and staggered potentials. By tuning these parameters, the system undergoes controlled topological phase transitions, leading to a quantized anomalous Hall effect without needing spin-orbit interaction. This provides a pathway for 'orbitronic' control over fundamental quantum properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates how orbital engineering can mimic the effects of spin-orbit coupling to realize topological states, which is highly relevant for designing next-generation electronic devices that rely on controlling band topology.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how orbital degrees of freedom can induce topological phase transitions, leading to Chern insulating behavior without requiring intrinsic spin-orbit coupling.

**Main result.** The system realizes a quantized anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}=e^2/h$) and Chern number ($C=1$) within a specific parameter window defined by the competition between orbital coupling ($\lambda$) and staggered potential ($\Delta$).

**Method.** The analysis uses a multi-orbital tight-binding model solved in momentum space, calculating topological invariants like the Berry curvature and Chern number via the Kubo formula.

**Model / system.** It models a two-dimensional lattice (like buckled honeycomb structures) using an extended Hamiltonian incorporating orbital coupling ($\lambda$) and a staggered potential ($\Delta$), which breaks both Time-Reversal and Inversion symmetry.

**Key observables.** Quantized anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$), Chern number ($C=1$), and the energy band structure $E_n(\mathbf{k})$ along high-symmetry paths.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative magnitudes of the orbital coupling ($\lambda$) and the staggered potential ($\Delta$) define the topological phase diagram, with critical points occurring at $|\lambda| = \pm\Delta$ and $\lambda = \pm\sqrt{\Delta^2+9t_0^{2}}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis neglects $p_z$ orbitals as they are assumed to be energetically far from the Fermi level, simplifying the model to in-plane $p$-orbitals.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy band structures showing gap closures at K/K' and $\Gamma$ points for varying parameters, and one figure shows the distribution of Berry curvature concentrating near specific valleys within the topological window.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the Hamiltonian using Slater-Koster formalism, derives the Bloch Hamiltonian in momentum space, analyzes the band structure evolution through parameter tuning ($\lambda$ vs $\Delta$), and finally calculates the Chern number to map out the phase diagram.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a theoretical study of orbital-induced topological phase transitions in a two-dimensional lattice model with staggered potential $(Δ)$ and orbital coupling $(λ)$ competing with the hopping strength. By tuning these parameters, two gap-closing mechanisms emerge: valley closure at $\mathbf{K}$ and $\mathbf{K'}$ for $λ=\pmΔ$, and a $\mathbfΓ$-point closure at $λ=\pm\sqrt{Δ^2+9t_0^{2}}$. Their interplay defines a topological window in which the Berry curvature localizes near a single valley, yielding a quantized anomalous Hall conductivity ($σ_{xy}=e^{2}/h$) and Chern number ($C=1$). These results demonstrate orbital-driven Chern insulating behavior without spin-orbit coupling. The resulting phase diagram captures the transition from trivial to topological phases and suggests practical routes for orbital engineering in tunable lattice systems.

</details>
