**Summary.** This work demonstrates that mechanically exfoliating the van der Waals ferromagnet $	ext{Fe}_3	ext{GaTe}_2$ induces a giant enhancement in its room-temperature magnetic coercivity. This effect is attributed to the transition from multi-domain behavior in bulk crystals to quasi-coherent, single-domain rotation in thin flakes. The findings position $	ext{Fe}_3	ext{GaTe}_2$ as a highly promising, rare-earth-free material for next-generation spintronic memory devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily condensed matter magnetism, the study of thickness-dependent magnetic transitions and the use of van der Waals heterostructures touches upon concepts relevant to engineered quantum interfaces and low-dimensional physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop rare-earth-free permanent magnets with high coercivity for spintronic applications, overcoming the limitations of bulk materials.

**Main result.** Mechanical exfoliation dramatically enhances the room-temperature coercive field in $	ext{Fe}_3	ext{GaTe}_2$ from negligible values to nearly 1 T when the field is applied in the plane.

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurements (magnetization, anomalous Hall effect) on exfoliated flakes with micromagnetic simulations to analyze the magnetic reversal mechanism.

**Model / system.** The system is $	ext{Fe}_3	ext{GaTe}_2$, a van der Waals ferromagnet. The analysis uses the Stoner-Wohlfarth model and micromagnetic theory to describe magnetization reversal, contrasting bulk domain-mediated behavior with thin-flake quasi-coherent rotation.

**Key observables.** Coercive field ($\mu_0H_c$), saturation magnetization ($M_s$), Anomalous Hall resistivity ($ho_{xy}^A$), and magnetic anisotropy constant ($K$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Thickness (down to $<100 	ext{ nm}$), temperature ($2 	ext{ K}$ to $300 	ext{ K}$), and field orientation relative to the crystal planes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The enhancement in coercivity is attributed to the system approaching a single-domain limit, governed by intrinsic magnetocrystalline anisotropy, rather than disorder-induced pinning mechanisms.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $\mu_0H_c$ increasing significantly for planar fields compared to c-axis fields upon exfoliation. Anomalous Hall response plots are used to analyze thickness dependence and distinguish intrinsic vs. extrinsic magnetic contributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for rare-earth-free magnets, presents experimental evidence showing coercivity enhancement via exfoliation (comparing bulk vs. thin flakes), analyzes this using micromagnetics/AHE measurements, and concludes that the material is promising for spintronics due to its tunable planar anisotropy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Permanent magnets with strong anisotropy and high coercivity underpin modern information and energy technologies, yet rare-earth-free alternatives remain limited. Here, we show that thickness engineering via mechanical exfoliation induces hard magnetic behavior in the van der Waals ferromagnet Fe$_3$GaTe$_2$. Bulk crystals exhibit Curie temperatures above 350 K but negligible room-temperature coercivity. When thinned below 100 nm, the coercive field is dramatically enhanced, reaching nearly 1 T at room temperature for in-plane fields which is comparable to values of conventional hard magnets. Micromagnetic analysis reveals a crossover in magnetization reversal from domain-mediated processes in bulk samples to quasi-coherent rotation in thin flakes, driven by increased effective anisotropy and suppressed domain formation. This thickness-dependent transition enables tuning of magnetic hardness without chemical modification. Combined with high saturation magnetization and robust room-temperature performance, Fe$_3$GaTe$_2$ emerges as a promising rare-earth-free material for spintronic applications. Its layered structure further allows integration into van der Waals heterostructures, where large in-plane coercivity can stabilize magnetic states against perturbations and interlayer coupling, offering potential for high-density nonvolatile memory and domain-wall-based devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, fully automated computational protocol for analyzing complex, spatially-resolved X-ray diffraction data from inhomogeneous materials like SOECs. By developing an open-access Python routine, the authors overcome significant manual analysis hurdles to map phase composition and strain across device cross-sections. This methodology is valuable for post mortem characterization of high-temperature electrochemical energy devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** Not directly relevant

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is automating the analysis of massive, spatially-resolved synchrotron diffraction datasets from chemically inhomogeneous polycrystalline materials.

**Main result.** The authors developed and applied a fully automated Python routine that successfully performs phase identification and Rietveld refinement across complex device cross-sections, enabling detailed post mortem characterization of degradation products in SOECs.

**Method.** The method involves an iterative, automated protocol combining peak matching, goodness-of-fit checks ($	ext{R}_{	ext{wp}}/	ext{R}_{	ext{exp}}$), and scale factor analysis within a Rietveld framework, implemented via Python scripts calling Topas.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Solid Oxide Electrolyser Cells (SOECs) fragments, which are analyzed using micron-scale X-ray diffraction ($\mu$-XRD). The technique is applicable to other electrochemical devices like all-solid-state batteries and fuel cells.

**Key observables.** Lattice parameters of various phases ($	ext{YSZ}$, $	ext{LSCF-6428}$), phase composition maps, strain distribution, and goodness-of-fit metrics across the sample cross-section.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Micron beam width, operating temperatures (high for SOECs), and crystallographic parameters derived from refinement ($	ext{R}_{	ext{wp}}/	ext{R}_{	ext{exp}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the automated protocol can reliably handle data complexity; specific limitations include assuming cubic unit cells when rhombohedral structures are expected, and fixing the zero error to avoid correlation issues.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate SOEC designs, pseudo-code for the phase identification algorithm, heatmaps showing variations in lattice parameters and zero error across the sample, and plots comparing fit quality metrics.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the $\mu$-XRD technique and its analytical bottleneck. It details the automated Python routine (including pseudocode) and applies it to an SOEC case study, presenting results on phase mapping, lattice parameter variations due to chemistry/strain, and limitations encountered during refinement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A novel approach to post mortem characterisation of electrochemical and photovoltaic devices is spatially-resolved diffraction using a hyper-focused, micron-width x-ray beam to examine the distribution of degradation products and strain, a technique called $μ$-XRD. Aside from the experimental difficulties associated with beam focusing and sample preparation, which are themselves non-trivial, the analysis of resulting data is complex and challenging, with full Rietveld analysis rarely attempted in literature. The difficulty lies in the size of the data, which may consist of hundreds or even thousands of diffraction patterns with very different crystallographic phase compositions depending on position within the device, and the difficulty in fitting the data due to the presence of many phases at the same position, including possible degradation products which may be difficult to index and assign to known phases. In this paper, we present a fully-automated open access Python routine for performing phase identification and Rietveld analysis on 2D datasets of diffraction pattern taken at micron-scale positions, measured over the cross-section of a chemically inhomogeneous device with polycrystalline phases. Solid oxide electrolyser cells are a promising technology for green hydrogen production which can utilise waste heat to split water at higher efficiencies than low-temperature electrolysis techniques such as polymer electrolyte membranes, but exhibit many degradation modes due to the high operating temperatures. We present a case study using our analysis protocol on an SOEC fragment encompassing the air electrode, cation diffusion barrier, electrolyte, and fuel electrode. With modification, this protocol could be applied to other devices such as all-solid-state batteries, wet-electrolyte battery electrodes, solid oxide fuel cells, photovoltaic devices, and metal-oxide pseudocapacitors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the successful synthesis of a novel ternary nitride, PrTaN2, as an epitaxial thin film using Molecular Beam Epitaxy. The authors characterized its crystal structure as orthorhombic with space group P222 through detailed XRD and STEM analysis. This work establishes MBE as a powerful technique for exploring complex, previously inaccessible nitride materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly materials-focused, the advanced use of structure factor analysis and systematic elimination of candidate phases mirrors complex pattern recognition problems relevant in quantum state tomography or phase diagram mapping.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was the discovery and structural characterization of a novel ternary nitride, PrTaN2, which is difficult to synthesize using conventional bulk methods.

**Main result.** The authors successfully synthesized PrTaN2 as a thin film via Molecular Beam Epitaxy (MBE) and determined its crystal structure to be orthorhombic with the space group P222.

**Method.** The study employed MBE for synthesis, followed by structural characterization using X-ray Diffraction (XRD), HAADF-STEM, and advanced structure factor fitting techniques.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a thin film of the ternary nitride PrTaN2 grown epitaxially on YAlO3 substrates. The analysis involves modeling crystal symmetry and determining atomic coordinates based on diffraction data.

**Key observables.** Lattice parameters (a, b, c), orthorhombic structure confirmation, space group identification (P222), and the ratio of structure factors ($F_{204}/F_{102}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The synthesis method utilizes an RF nitrogen radical source for highly nitriding conditions; structural analysis relies on Wyckoff position constraints.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial assumptions regarding atomic positions were tested against experimental data, and the final structure determination relied on systematically excluding alternative candidate phases based on diffraction rules.

**Figures summary.** Figures show HAADF-STEM images confirming cation ordering (Pr vs Ta), FFT patterns matching XRD periodicity, and multiple XRD scans used to confirm the orthorhombic lattice parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a progression from synthesis via MBE to structural confirmation using STEM/XRD, followed by rigorous structure factor fitting that systematically rules out competing phases before finalizing the space group assignment.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report the discovery of a novel ternary nitride PrTaN$_2$ synthesized as a thin film using molecular beam epitaxy. The combination of e-beam evaporation for refractory elements and a radio-frequency nitrogen radical source enables growth under a highly nitriding environment, providing access to phases not readily obtained in bulk synthesis. Structural characterization by X-ray diffraction and high-angle annular dark-field scanning transmission electron microscopy reveals that the compound crystallizes in an orthorhombic structure and grows with a well-defined orientation on YAlO$_3$ substrates, while remaining essentially strain-free. To determine the crystal structure from limited thin-film diffraction data, we developed a fitting procedure based on structure factors. By combining extinction rules with constraints from Wyckoff positions, the number of fitting parameters is significantly reduced, enabling reliable structure determination. Systematic exclusion of alternative candidate phases in the Pr-Ta-N system, together with structure factor fitting, identifies the space group as $P222$ and determines the atomic coordinates. The present results demonstrate that thin-film growth with molecular beam epitaxy can stabilize previously unexplored ternary nitrides, and establish a practical approach for structural determination in such systems. This work provides a pathway for the exploration of new complex nitride materials.

</details>
